   `positionJson`, and the server clears the text position when it is absent — every page load could
   wipe it.

**Smaller data fixes (#718).** Double-submit of a vocabulary save or add-to-library no longer 500s on
the unique index (23505 → re-read the winner, normal 200). `/me/library` and `/me/progress` no longer
cut silently at 50 rows — no client paged, so a big library just looked shorter.

**Lesson.** Each pass of highlight sync was right for the case it was written for and wrong for the
next device. The fix that held was the general one — a three-way merge against a base — not one more
rule.

<a id="2026-10-05-security-ops-hardening"></a>

## Security & ops hardening from the architecture review — backend, infra — 2026-10-05

All P0/P1 items of the [architecture review 2026-10](../01-architecture/review-2026-10/00-summary.md),
in one day.

**Security.**
- **Stored files (#690).** `/storage/` and the API's own static files send a `sandbox` CSP +
  `nosniff`, so an uploaded file cannot run as a page on our origin.
- **`/internal` (#690).** nginx returns 404 for `/api/internal…` on every host (pollers call the API
  directly, not through nginx); checked with `/api/Internal`, `//internal`, `%69nternal`. The two
  copies of the "is this a local request" check became one tested `InternalNetwork` helper.
- **Copyrighted PDFs (#690)** removed from the repo (git history still has them — the owner's call).
- **Connect keys (#693)** (`tsk_`) get the same account-route scope as OAuth tokens: no wipe-all
  vocabulary, no `/auth/me`, no profile.
- **Rate-limit keys (#693).** One `IpPartitionKey` for all 22 per-IP partitions; IPv6 is grouped by
  /64, because one client usually holds a whole /64 and could rotate addresses past the limit. nginx
  keys on `CF-Connecting-IP` only when the peer is the local tunnel.
- **Tokens (#704).** Refresh tokens stored as SHA-256; migration `HashRefreshTokens` hashes existing
  rows in place (the SQL is pinned equal to the C# helper by a test), so sessions survived the deploy.
  Reuse of a rotated token after a 30 s grace revokes its successor. Access tokens carry an audience
  (`textstack-user` / `textstack-admin`), so admin refuses a user token. Rollback signs everyone out —
  written in the migration.
- **Uploads / EPUB (#704).** Quota checked from the stream length before reading; file names that
  resolve outside the book folder are refused; `EpubArchiveGuard` refuses > 10,000 entries or
  > 500 MB declared size (zip bomb) as a parse error, not a crash.

**Ops.**
- **Health check (#691)** asked `/health`, which nginx served from the SPA — 200 `text/html`, so a
  dead API passed. It now asks `/api/health` and checks for `"healthy"`. Backups run `pg_dump | gzip`
  under `pipefail` and check the dump trailer; a daily disk alarm fires at 85%.
- **Claude CLI pollers (#691)** run through `infra/scripts/claude-isolated.sh`: fresh temp dir,
  `--tools ""`.
- **SSG off the deploy path (#697).** Every deploy ran a full rebuild (~2000 routes, ~25 min) and held
  the only self-hosted runner for 35–50 min. Now it runs nightly after the backup; deploys keep the
  old tree, and a `rebuild_ssg` input exists for releases that change SEO rendering. Trade-off: after
  a frontend release, crawlers see the old rendering until the night.
- **Deploy vs a running rebuild (#698).** The same day, two rebuilds were rendering while deploys
  emptied `dist/`. The 2026-08-31 guard correctly refused to promote a half tree — then #691's new
  gate failed the deploy on ssg-worker's "last rebuild failed". Now a deploy waits for an active
  rebuild (≤ 40 min, only jobs from the last hour — a crashed `Running` row must not cost every
  deploy 40 min), and the gate ignores ssg-worker content state (`health-check.yml` alarms on it).
- **The flaky test that failed deploys (#699).** `ConnectedApps` failed three deploys. The mock's `t`
  was a new function each render and the component reloads when `t` changes, so a late reload put a
  removed row back. A 3 s timeout had "fixed" it before. Now a stable `t`, plus an assertion that the
  load runs once — which fails with the old mock.

**Lesson.** A new safety check meets old state first: #691's gate was right and still failed a good
deploy, because nobody had asked what "unhealthy" means for a content worker.

<a id="2026-10-05-backups-r2-restore-drill"></a>

## Backups: off-site to R2, deduplicated, and a restore drill — infra, docs — 2026-10-05

**Why.** Review P0 #4: every backup lived on the same disk as the data. P1 #9: no backup had ever been
restored, and the DR runbook failed as written.

- **Off-site (#696).** Nightly restic (Docker image, nothing installed on the host) to Cloudflare R2:
  DB dump, `data/storage`, `.env`. Encrypted client-side; 7 nightly + 3 monthly; fails above 9 GB to
  stay in the free 10 GB. Local copies 5 → 2, freeing ~8 GB.
- **Dedup (#700).** The first run from `main` added 1.26 GB — the whole dump. A `.gz` differs
  byte-for-byte every night, so restic cannot deduplicate it, and the 9 GB guard would have tripped
  within a week. Now the dump goes to restic **uncompressed** via stdin; restic chunks and compresses
  it itself. Local proof: one changed row → 180 KiB stored, vs 1.5 MB for the gz.
- **Restore drill (#703).** `.github/workflows/restore-drill.yml`, monthly and on demand, on a **clean
  GitHub-hosted runner** — nothing from the prod box. It restores the latest snapshot, checks core
  tables are not truncated, checks 200 random covers exist, checks `.env` keys by name. First run: DB
  206 s (65 tables, 1,423 editions, 40,582 chapters), files 37 s (22,449 files, 4.3 GB), covers
  200/200. The DR steps in [`backup.md`](../03-ops/backup.md) were rewritten from what the drill
  actually ran.

**Lesson.** The compression that saves space locally is what breaks deduplication remotely. And a
runbook is a guess until a machine with nothing on it follows it.

<a id="2026-10-05-local-llm-quality"></a>

## Local LLM quality: language names, Ollama upgrade, versioned cache keys — backend, infra — 2026-10-05

**Why.** A local-model comparison showed vocabulary explanations coming back in **English** for most
non-English readers.

- **Root cause: the prompt (#712).** Prompts said "write in `uk`". `gemma4:e2b` does not map an ISO
  code to a language reliably; with "Ukrainian" it was right 9/9. New
  `Domain/Utilities/LanguageNames.ToEnglishName` (all 88 codes, parity test against
  `packages/shared/src/languages.ts`), used by distractors/explanations, cluster titles, tags,
  Explain, Translate and the SEO crews. Also: the distractor prompt lost its example words (the model
  copied them into answers), the tag parser takes the first balanced JSON array, and Ollama replies
  lose a leaked `<think>` block.
- **Ollama 0.23.1 → 0.35.1 (#711).** Prod had run gemma4 since May on an Ollama older than gemma4
  needs (≥ 0.30.9), and the model was pulled once by hand. Deploy now re-pulls the configured model
  (no-op when current, non-fatal). The upgrade alone did **not** fix the language bug.
- **Cache keys (#718).** Explain/translate answers are cached 30 days under a key with no prompt
  version, so #712 would have reached readers only a month later. Keys now carry
  `v{PromptVersion}|{model}|`.

**Lesson.** When a small model answers in the wrong language, read what the prompt actually says
before changing the model. A cache key must include everything that changes the answer.

<a id="2026-10-05-reader-bars-and-update-banner"></a>

## Reader bars hide again; mobile shows its updates — web, mobile — 2026-10-05

- **Bars (#701).** Owner: "the bars used to hide when scrolling down". After a scroll-up revealed them,
  every small downward event reset the 48 px baseline, so slow, trackpad or finger scrolling never hid
  them. Same code on web (`useImmersiveMode`) and in the mobile WebView detector (`readerBridge.ts`).
  The branch is deleted; a revealed bar now stays until you scroll down. Tests fail on the old code.
  Found, not fixed: web tap-on-text never reveals the bars since #157 (it would collide with word-tap
  — a product call).
- **Update banner (#702).** An OTA used to download silently and the app just blinked on reload. Now:
  a thin progress bar while downloading (not inside the reader), a one-off toast if an update waits
  for you to leave the reader, "Restarting to apply the update…" for 0.8 s, and "TextStack updated"
  once after. The first OTA carrying this code shows nothing; the next one does.

<a id="2026-10-05-architecture-review-and-p2-batch"></a>

## Architecture review 2026-10 and the first P2 batch — backend, web, mobile, infra, docs — 2026-10-05

**The review (#705).** [`docs/01-architecture/review-2026-10/`](../01-architecture/review-2026-10/00-summary.md):
system map (C4), backend, ops and client reviews, ranked gaps P0–P3 with a status table. The security
section is not committed (public repo). All P0/P1 items were fixed the same day — see
[Security & ops hardening](#2026-10-05-security-ops-hardening) and
[Backups](#2026-10-05-backups-r2-restore-drill).

**Podcast deleted (#692).** Live, undocumented, unused. Worker, script builder, ffmpeg assembly,
endpoints, web player, admin button; `podcast_generation_jobs` dropped (`DropPodcast`), ffmpeg out of
the Worker image. Old mp3s stay on disk.

**P2 batch 1 (#706) and 1b (#707)** — small items, each a real bug:
- Catalog ingestion had no retry cap: a job that crashed the worker was re-picked every 10 min
  forever. Now 3 attempts, then Failed; #707 also fails jobs left `Queued` at the cap.
- `book_collections` rows were orphaned on upload delete, library removal and edition delete, and
  adding checked no ownership. Fixed, plus data migration `CleanupOrphanedBookCollections`.
- Migrations ran twice — in `migrator` and again at API start, which undid a `MIGRATE_TARGET`
  rollback. Now only the migrator (the API opts in for local `dotnet run`).
- Password reset also revokes MCP keys and OAuth grants; expired refresh tokens are deleted daily.
- Clearing a highlight note never reached the server (`removeNote` is now sent).
- Deploy gate waits up to 90 s for `starting` services; `make deploy` removed (use the workflow).
- Not done: `claude-isolated.sh` under `env -i` — only a try on the server shows whether the CLI still
  authenticates.

**Advisory (#720).** GHSA-68fv (source-map-js) failed every PR. It arrives only through build/test
tooling, and taking the fix is a lockfile change that can move the Expo fingerprint, so it is
allowlisted and rides the next dependency refresh.

<a id="2026-10-04-offer-save-words"></a>

## MCP — the assistant offers to save words — 2026-10-04

The vocabulary tools shipped on 2026-10-03, and the next day's reading session showed the gap:
ChatGPT read the insights and saved a chapter review, but never offered a single word — the six
words saved from *Disgrace* that afternoon were all reader taps. Nothing told it to.

Prompt-only fix, the same lever that already makes it offer the chapter review: the server
`instructions` (`McpBridgeCore.Instructions`) now say to remember words the reader asked about or
clearly did not know, offer them once, together, at the end (or with the review offer), skip any
already saved, and call `add_vocabulary_words` only on yes. The tool description carries one
clause of the same, because ChatGPT leans on descriptions; `ReviewMethod.md` gets one line so the
review flow offers them too. No new tool, no API change; the method goes to v4 (its pinned hash forces a bump on any edit;
the saved review's shape did not change).

<a id="2026-10-03-mcp-vocabulary-write"></a>

## MCP — the assistant can add, edit and delete vocabulary words — backend, web — 2026-10-03

The owner reads with ChatGPT and Claude connected over MCP. Asked to save unknown words, the assistant
could not: the only vocabulary tool was the read-only `list_my_vocabulary`, which did not even return
ids. The vocabulary API already did everything, so this is mostly bridge work (18 → 21 tools):

- `add_vocabulary_words` — up to 20 words per call, each with language and a translation **or** a
  definition (the review card needs one prompt: a translation for a different native language, a short
  definition when the reader reads their own language — owner's call), optional sentence, optionally linked to `bookId`/`editionId` (not both).
  The bridge calls `POST /me/vocabulary/words` once per word, in order, and returns one line per word
  in fixed wording: added (first review today), already in vocabulary (current translation X — use
  `update_vocabulary_word`), queued for tomorrow (daily cap; not editable until then), kept as
  reference only (rare-word filter on), or the server's reason for a refusal. A refused word does not
  stop the batch; a 429 does, and the rest are reported as not attempted. The whole array is validated
  before any HTTP call.
- `update_vocabulary_word` (`PATCH`, at least one of translation/definition) and
  `delete_vocabulary_word` (`DELETE`, one word; the description says only on a clear request). A 404
  points the model at `list_my_vocabulary`, which now returns each word's `id`.
- API hardening, no migration: a save with no `nativeLanguage` falls back to `users.native_language`
  (still 400 if both are empty); a save authenticated by a connect key or OAuth token is tagged
  `source = "mcp"` server-side on both the SRS and pending rows (the stdio/device-JWT path still says
  `tap`); word POST/PATCH/DELETE share the per-user `highlight-write` limit (120/min); wipe-all
  `DELETE /me/vocabulary/words` refuses OAuth tokens. The OAuth consent screen now says the assistant
  can save vocabulary words.
- Bridge: `ReadResultAsync` treats 204 as success, carries the status, and reads `message`, ProblemDetails
  `detail`, a bare `error` code or a JSON-string body, so the server's reason reaches the model.

Not built (add when asked): a mark-known tool, a `nativeLanguage` tool input, chapter linkage (the model
holds slugs, not ids), editing pending words, bulk delete.

<a id="2026-10-03-drop-free-dictionary"></a>

## Reader — free dictionary removed — backend, web, mobile — 2026-10-03

The free Free Dictionary API (api.dictionaryapi.dev) was the one third party on the reading path we
could not rely on: outages (the 2026-09-05 one turned word taps into 3–10 s spinners) and false 404s,
which our own negative cache then stored for 24 h as "not found". It is gone entirely:

- Backend: `GET /dictionary/{lang}/{word}`, `DictionaryCache` + `DictionaryCacheSweeper`, the
  `dictionary` rate-limit policy, the `Dictionary` config section, the `data/dictionary-cache` volume
  (compose, Makefile, CI). `DefinitionEnricher` is deleted too — new vocabulary words no longer get an
  automatic English definition on save (existing data untouched, no migration); the distractor
  generator just gets the client-supplied definition, if any. The admin-only
  `POST /admin/vocabulary/backfill-definitions` went with it (nothing in the admin app called it).
- Shared: `dictionaryApi` client and the `privacy.thirdPartiesDictionary` line on the privacy page.
- Web: word popup drops phonetic + dictionary definition; translation stays. In **definition mode**
  (confirmed native language = book language, so there is nothing to translate) the popup fetches
  the contextual Explain (`POST /explain`, word + its sentence) into the same slot, with the same
  loading state and auto-dismiss rescheduling. Explain is anonymous (per-IP 20/min); any failure
  shows nothing. The IndexedDB `dictionary` store is left in the schema (no version bump) and is
  drained by the existing 30-day TTL sweep. Home FAQ no longer promises "a dictionary definition".
- Mobile + web review feedback: no live lookup; the card's own `definition` is shown when present.

Explain stays on gpt-4.1-mini: a nano switch was considered for cost and rejected — at current
traffic the difference is cents a month, and Explain now also runs on every same-language tap.

<a id="2026-10-01-one-discuss"></a>

## Library — one Discuss: conversation first, the review at the end — backend, web, mobile — 2026-10-01

Two entries confused readers: "Discuss this book" opened a free chat whose result stayed in the chat,
and "Review chapter" ran the method and brought structure back. The owner's call: one entry. The
reader presses Discuss on a chapter; the chat opens with "I'm reading <book>, at the chapter <x>.
Let's talk about it." and the id line (catalog books now carry their slug too). The conversation is
free. The MCP server instructions now say spoilers are soft — before a chapter the reader hasn't
reached, the assistant says so and asks — and that when the talk winds down it offers "Review this
chapter and save it to TextStack?", running get_chapter_review → save_chapter_review only on yes.
Conclusions about other parts go to save_insight at book level. Review method v3 adds "If you have
already been talking": blocks come from the chapter and the conversation, the reader's own
confusions are the best problems, nothing already answered is asked again.

"Discuss the book" remains only when the reader hasn't started (no current chapter). The book-page
menu shows exactly one discuss item; chapter rows say Discuss; the web reader has a "✦ Discuss this
chapter" pill at the end of each discussable chapter (or ✓ Reviewed · Open review); mobile offers it
on leaving a finished chapter. The server's reading frontier on get/save_chapter_review is
unchanged: reviewing a chapter you haven't read makes no sense, and the 409 already tells the
assistant to confirm and call set_book_progress.

<a id="2026-09-30-one-assistant-button"></a>

## Library — one Assistant button on the book page — web, mobile — 2026-09-30

A book page had two ways into the reader's assistant that behaved differently: a "Talk this book
over" block with two raw links (Open in Claude / Open in ChatGPT) and a hint about "connecting
TextStack as a connector", and the chapter-row Review button, which knows which chat is connected
(`/me/oauth/grants`) and offers a connect dialog when none is. The links opened a chat whether or not
it could reach TextStack.

**What changed**

- **One button.** "✦ Assistant ▾" beside Continue / Start Reading on all four detail screens
  (web `BookDetailPage`, `UserBookDetailPage`; mobile `book/[slug]`, `my-books/[id]`). Items:
  *Discuss this book* (the humanized brief) and *Review current chapter* with the chapter's title
  under it — the chapter of the reader's progress. Hidden with no progress, for a slugless chapter,
  or for front matter; *Open review of current chapter* → its summary when it already has a review
  (`currentReviewChapter`, shared).
- **One launcher.** `useAssistantLauncher` (web + mobile) now owns the grants cache, the remembered
  Claude/ChatGPT choice and the connect-dialog state; `ReviewChapterButton` and the menu both use it.
  One connected → opens it; both → remembered one, with a "Chat in: Claude | ChatGPT" toggle inside
  the menu (the chapter row keeps its ▾); none → the existing connect dialog/sheet. Signed out / guest
  → the same dialog.
- **Web**: the menu fetches grants only when opened — the catalog page is prerendered and mostly
  seen signed out, where a mount-time call is a 401. **Mobile**: the menu expands inline under the
  button row rather than in a modal, because the connect sheet is itself a Modal and opening one
  while another is dismissing is unreliable on iOS.
- **Removed**: `DiscussWithAssistant` (both apps), `library.discuss.*` keys; new `library.assistant.*`.

- **Review item** follows the chapter rows' rule (`isReviewableChapter`, #657): hidden for front/back
  matter and chapters under 800 words, unless the chapter already has a review.
- **Connect dialog** lead is now general, since Discuss reaches it too: "TextStack works inside your
  own Claude or ChatGPT — connect it once, it takes a minute." (owner's wording).
- The button is shown to guests and crawlers, prerendered pages included (owner decision).

<a id="2026-09-30-human-handoff-briefs"></a>

## MCP — the message our buttons send reads like a person wrote it — backend, web, mobile, worker — 2026-09-30

The owner opened "Discuss with assistant" on his phone and ChatGPT showed him, prefilled:
"…If you have the TextStack connector, this book is bookId a1751e58-…. Read it with get_my_book
and get_my_chapter, and check get_my_insights first… write the conclusions back with save_insight…".
That was a reasonable message when the connector was the exception. Since OAuth made it one click it
is the normal case, and the instructions were talking to the model through the reader's mouth.

**What changed**

- **Server instructions.** The MCP server now returns `instructions` at `initialize`
  (`McpBridgeCore.Instructions`, both stdio and http): find the book (id line, else
  `search_my_library`/`get_my_reading`), check `get_my_insights` first, read, save with `save_insight`,
  review via `get_chapter_review` → `save_chapter_review`, never run ahead of the reader. Tool
  descriptions are untouched, so the manifest drift tests are too.
- **Briefs.** Discuss: `Let's discuss "AI Engineering" by Chip Huyen in TextStack.` (+ progress /
  chapter) and `(TextStack: book <id>)`. Review: `Let's review the chapter "…" of "…" in TextStack.`
  and `(TextStack: book <id>, chapter <slug>)`. A catalog book carries `catalog <slug>, edition <id>`,
  still both, for the same reason as before. Titles are clipped so the id line survives the 1200-char
  cap — the Discuss brief used to cut from the end, which is where the id was.
- **Title junk.** `BookTitleCleaner` strips a trailing `(…)`/`[…]` group whose every comma-separated
  item is a lowercase domain — `(z-library.sk, 1lib.sk, z-lib.sk)`, `[libgen.rs]`. `(Node.js)`,
  `(ASP.NET)`, `(2nd Edition)` survive. Applied to the extracted title (as before) and now also to the
  filename placeholder, which a PDF with no metadata title keeps forever; the ingestion check that
  swaps the placeholder for the extracted title accepts both forms.

**Not done**: existing titles are not rewritten. The owner's AI Engineering upload needs a one-time
rename in the app. The manifest endpoint does not expose the instructions (it would mean mirroring
one more string into Contracts for no current reader).
<a id="2026-09-30-chapter-review-reader"></a>

## Library — chapter review reaches the reader and Practice — web (+ mobile) — 2026-09-30

PR 3 of [chapter-review.md](../05-features/chapter-review.md) (slices E+F). No backend change.

**Reviewed badge.** `ReaderPage` reads the book's insights once (`useBookReviews`, the same deduped
`/me/insights` call the book page uses) and turns them into highlight id → {chapter, block title, rule}
(`reviewedHighlightMarks`, shared). A React context hands that map to four consumers instead of a prop
threaded through `ReaderHighlights`/`PdfOriginalView`/`PdfPage`. HTML reader: a second Overlayer
namespace `reviewed-mark` with a custom `DrawFn` — a small dot at the top-right of the highlight's last
rect — and no engine change; the click handler accepts either namespace, because the mark shares the
highlight's hit rects and is the topmost entry. PDF Original: `PdfHighlightLayer` paints a
`.pdf-hl-dot` after the last rect, scaled; it is not a `.pdf-hl-rect`, so hit-testing is unchanged.
Tapping the highlight opens the existing popup (`NoteEditor` / `PdfHighlightPopup`) with a row on top:
"✓ Reviewed · block", "★ rule", "Open review →" to the summary; note, recolor and delete stay.

**Quote → text.** Each highlight quote on the summary page links to the reader with
`?direct=1&highlight=<id>` — catalog `/books/:slug/:chapter`, upload `/library/my/:id/read/:chapter`,
and for a PDF anchor the chapterless Original route, which scrolls to the anchor's page. (The
HighlightsPage catalog link uses `/books/:slug/read/:chapter`, which is not a route; not changed here.)

**Chapter questions.** A card on Practice (`/vocabulary`) — "Chapter questions · N due · Start" —
from `GET /me/review-questions/due?limit=1`'s `totalDue`; hidden while nothing is due, and also shown
on the page's no-words empty state, because a reader can have questions without saved words. The
session, `/:lang/review/questions` (signed-in, noindex, not prerendered): prompt → Show answer →
answer, ★ rule, book · chapter → Forgot / Almost / Knew → `POST /me/review-questions/{id}/answer` →
next, "3 / 6", then an end screen. A failed answer stays on the card and says so. Word SRS untouched.

**No Review on service sections.** `isReviewableChapter` (shared): false for Cover, Copyright,
Contents, Index, About the Author(s), Acknowledg(e)ments, Dedication, Colophon, Also by…, Glossary,
Bibliography, References, Notes (whole title, case-insensitive, optional "12." / "IV –" / "Chapter 3:"
numbering) and for chapters under 800 words; unknown word count keeps the button. A reviewed chapter
still shows "✓ Reviewed →". Applied in both apps' chapter-row component.

Strings: `chapterReview.questions.*` in the shared catalogue (mobile's PR 4 reuses them); both goldens
edited by hand.

<a id="2026-09-30-chapter-review-ui"></a>

## Library — chapter review gets its button and its page — web, mobile — 2026-09-30

PR 2 of [chapter-review.md](../05-features/chapter-review.md) (slices C+D). PR 1 gave the reader's
assistant two MCP tools; nothing in the app could start a review or show one. No backend change.

**The button lives on every chapter row**, on all four book screens (web catalog + upload, mobile
catalog + upload). A reviewed chapter shows "✓ Reviewed →" to its summary; the rest show a small
Review. Which chat opens is read from `GET /me/oauth/grants`: one of Claude/ChatGPT connected → it
opens directly; both → the remembered one (a two-item menu the first time), with a small ▾ (mobile: a
chevron) beside the button to pick the other — the pick is remembered per device (`localStorage` /
AsyncStorage, key `chapterReview.assistant`) and only counts while that grant exists;
none → a connect dialog with the connect page's one-step text, Copy URL and a link there, never a chat
that cannot reach TextStack. The brief (`buildChapterReviewBrief`, shared, ≤1200 chars with titles
clipped so the ids always survive) names the book, author, chapter, the bookId or editionId and the
chapterSlug, and tells the assistant to call `get_chapter_review`, follow the method exactly and save
with `save_chapter_review`.

**Web: the grants are fetched once per page and held synchronously**, so the click calls
`window.open` inside the user gesture — an open after an `await` is what popup blockers eat. Mobile
fetches on press (the OS link opener does not care) and surfaces a failed `Linking.openURL` as a toast.

**The summary page** — `/:lang/books/:slug/review/:chapterSlug` and
`/:lang/library/my/:id/review/:chapterSlug` (signed-in, noindex, not in `/ssg/routes`; nginx already
sends `noindex` for `/en/books/*/*`), and `app/chapter-review.tsx` on mobile. One card per block:
problem, why, the rule highlighted to memorize, the reader's highlight quotes resolved against their
highlights ("highlight removed" when one was deleted; nothing is labelled removed if the highlights
call fails), and the question with a Show/Hide answer. Then recall, applications, open threads, and
closed threads — whose text is looked up in the earlier review that opened them. Review again and
Next (the next chapter's summary if reviewed, otherwise its Review) at the bottom. Titles only; the
chapter number never appears. `BookInsightsSection` (both clients) links a reviewed row to its page.

**One `/me/insights` request per book page.** The chapter list and `BookInsightsSection` both need
it; the client now shares an in-flight request per book (web and shared client), cleared on settle
so nothing is served stale.

**A flaky test was a real flash.** `useBookReviews` set `loading` in its effect, so on the render
where the book first arrived it was still false with no insights, and the summary page painted "not
reviewed yet" plus a Review button for a chapter that has a review — one frame, and exactly the frame
a test's heading query sometimes resolved on. `loading` is now derived from which book the data
belongs to; a test checks the DOM from inside the fetch call and fails on the old hook.

**Not done here.** Tap-a-quote-to-open-the-reader (quotes are shown, not linked) — PR 3.

<a id="2026-09-29-mcp-oauth-connect-in-one-click"></a>

## MCP — connect Claude/ChatGPT by signing in, not by pasting a key (OAuth) — backend, web, mobile, infra — 2026-09-29

Connecting an assistant meant creating a `tsk_` key and pasting it — as a header for Claude, inside
a URL for ChatGPT. Readwise's connector is the bar: paste the URL (or pick it from the directory),
sign in, approve. Both directories require OAuth for user data.

**Frontend** (owner-approved layout):

- **Consent page** `/:lang/oauth/consent?req=` (`apps/web/src/pages/OAuthConsentPage.tsx`) — "<client>
  wants to connect to your TextStack library", what it can and cannot do, signed-in email with a
  *switch* (sign out → sign in, same URL), and **always the redirect host** ("Returns to: claude.ai"),
  the one thing a phishing request cannot fake. Allow/Cancel navigate the browser to the `redirect`
  the API returns. Signed out → the normal auth modal, back on the same `req`. A guest never calls
  approve: "Create an account to connect an assistant — your books are kept" (registration promotes
  the row in place, ADR-014); a `403 account_required` from approve lands on the same state.
  Expired/used/unknown request → "This request expired — start the connection again". noindex, not in
  `/ssg/routes`.
- **Connect page** (web `/mcp`, mobile `connect.tsx`): the main content is now one step — paste
  `https://textstack.app/mcp` into Claude (Settings → Connectors → Add custom connector) or ChatGPT,
  sign in, Allow — then **Connected apps** (`GET /me/oauth/grants`: name, returns-to host, connected,
  last used, **Disconnect** → `DELETE`). The key UI (create, Claude Desktop config, ChatGPT personal
  URL, revoke) moved unchanged into a collapsed **For developers** section, with the local-tool
  config. Stale "13 tools" → 18, and the remote copy that said catalog tools work without sign-in
  is gone (every `/mcp` request now needs a bearer).
- Shared: `OAuthGrant` type + `oauthGrantsApi` (mobile) in `packages/shared`; the web keeps its own
  cookie-auth copy (`apps/web/src/api/oauth.ts`), and `noSharedApiOnWeb` now also guards
  `mcpKeysApi`/`oauthGrantsApi`.

**Backend:**

**What was built** ([ADR-017](../01-architecture/adr/ADR-017-mcp-oauth-authorization-server.md)):

- **An OAuth 2.1 authorization server inside the API** (`Api/Endpoints/OAuthEndpoints.cs`): RFC 8414
  metadata, authorize (PKCE S256 only, `resource` must be `https://textstack.app/mcp`), a consent
  request the SPA renders, approve/deny, token (form-urlencoded; code + refresh), DCR, RFC 7009
  revoke, and `GET/DELETE /me/oauth/grants` for "Connected apps". Public clients only. CIMD clients
  (client_id = an https URL) are fetched behind an SSRF guard that checks every resolved address in
  the socket connect callback, so DNS rebinding cannot slip a private IP in after the check.
- **Opaque hashed tokens**: `tso_` access (1 h), `tsr_` refresh (90 days sliding, rotated on every
  use — the old one then matches no row). `McpKeyAuthMiddleware` learned the prefix: one query checks
  hash, revocation, expiry and audience. Revoking a grant stops the next request.
- **Login for all of `/mcp`**: no bearer → `401` with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata=…`,
  which is the signal that starts the sign-in; the MCP host serves the protected-resource metadata.
  Anonymous catalog tools over MCP are gone, by owner decision. `tsk_` keys and `/mcp/k/<key>` are
  untouched. An expired `tso_` also gets a `401` (`error="invalid_token"`) so clients refresh
  instead of failing every tool call — the failure Readwise's connector is known for.
- **Guests cannot authorize** (`403 account_required`), same distinction as `RequireAiAccount`.
- **Owner decisions after review**: a `tso_` token is library-only — account-management groups
  refuse it with `403 insufficient_scope` (`OAuthTokenPolicy`, applied per route group); reconnecting
  the same app replaces its grant; reusing a rotated refresh token revokes the whole grant.
- **nginx** routes the three well-known paths and `/oauth/` explicitly; before, they answered the
  SPA's `200 text/html`, which a client would try to parse as JSON.
- **Tool annotations**: every tool has a `title` and `readOnlyHint`/`destructiveHint`/
  `openWorldHint` (directory review requires them). `ReadOnly` is a required property, so a new tool
  cannot ship without deciding.

**Migration** `AddOAuth`: `oauth_clients`, `oauth_authorization_requests`, `oauth_grants` (explicit
names — the snake_case convention would have produced `o_auth_`).

**Verified** against a branch API on an isolated Postgres + the branch MCP host: the curl chain
401 → PRM → AS metadata → register → authorize → approve → token → `/mcp` 200 → revoke → `/mcp` 401
`invalid_token`; `tsk_` header and `/mcp/k/` still 200; nginx `-t` and a live run of the new
locations. Mutation checks: disabling the PKCE compare or the audience clause fails tests.

**Not verified**: a real claude.ai / ChatGPT connection (needs the deployed endpoint), CIMD against a
real client's document, the mobile Connected apps screen on a device (tsc + unit only).

**Verified (frontend)** in a browser against the branch API + throwaway Postgres: DCR client →
`/oauth/authorize` → consent page (signed out: sign-in prompt) → test-login → consent → Allow →
`redirect_uri?code&state&iss` → code exchanged for a `tso_` token; `/en/mcp` lists the grant,
Disconnect empties it on the server; the developer section still creates keys.

<a id="2026-09-29-uploads-pdf-chapters-under-parts"></a>

## Uploads — PDF chapters found under parts, not replaced by them — worker — 2026-09-29

Chapter review (ADR-016) reviews one chapter. On the owner's copy of *Designing Data-Intensive
Applications* — a PDF upload — a "chapter" was a whole part: "Part I. Foundations of Data Systems",
pages 23–166. The book had 8 chapters on record, and three of them were the parts.

`PdfChapterDetector` builds chapters from the PDF outline, top level only. That rule exists for a good
reason: flattening the outline conflates chapters with their sections, and an O'Reilly book comes out
as 30+ "chapters". But DDIA's outline has one more level — parts on top, chapters beneath, sections
beneath those — so top-level-only picked the parts.

**Fix.** Selection is now a pure function over a plain outline tree (`SelectChapters`), and a
top-level entry is expanded into its children when it reads like a part ("Part I", "Частина 2",
"Book III"), or when at least two of its children read like chapters ("Chapter 5", "5. Replication") —
the second catches books whose part titles carry no "Part" word. Sections ("Leaders and Followers")
read like neither, so an ordinary chapter root stays whole. A part's opening pages are folded into its
first chapter instead of becoming a two-page chapter of their own.

Checked against the owner's actual file: 12 chapters, "Chapter 1. Reliable, Scalable, and
Maintainable Applications" (pp. 23–48) through "Chapter 12. The Future of Data Systems" (pp. 511–574),
plus preface and back matter. Unit tests cover the DDIA shape, parts without the word "Part",
O'Reilly chapter-with-sections roots, a part with one child, and a page-less root; removing the
expansion branch fails two of them.

**Existing uploads keep their chapters** until their owner re-extracts (`POST /me/books/{id}/retry`,
which re-extracts Ready books too). No mass re-extraction: new slugs would orphan other readers'
insights and bookmarks keyed by chapter slug. PDF highlights and PDF progress are page-anchored and
survive a re-extract.

<a id="2026-09-29-mcp-chapter-review"></a>

## MCP — chapter review: the reader's assistant reviews a chapter, and the result comes home as structure — backend — 2026-09-29

Stage 2 of TextStack × ChatGPT/Claude, slices A+B of
[chapter-review.md](../05-features/chapter-review.md) (storage decision:
[ADR-016](../01-architecture/adr/ADR-016-chapter-review-lives-in-book-insight.md)). We run no LLM:
the reader's own assistant does the reviewing, over MCP, by a method we serve. No UI yet — the button,
the summary page, reader badges and the Practice section are PRs 2–4.

**Two tools (16 → 18).** `get_chapter_review` returns everything in one call: the method
(`Application/ChapterReview/ReviewMethod.md`, embedded, versioned — editing it is an API deploy, not
an MCP rebuild), the chapter's stored plain text split into 40k-char parts at paragraph boundaries
(losslessly — `get_my_chapter` silently truncates a DDIA chapter today), the reader's highlights in
that chapter (PDF highlights placed by page against `SourceStartPage..SourceEndPage`), their saved
words from the book, threads left open by earlier chapters, and any previous review.
`save_chapter_review` validates and saves.

**A refusal lists every problem.** The validator is pure and collects all errors with a path and a
code (`blocks[1].rootCause: must be one line`), so a model fixes everything in one retry. The bridge
used to collapse every non-2xx into null; these two tools relay the server's message verbatim —
including the spoiler gate's instruction to confirm with the reader and call `set_book_progress`.

**Spoiler gate.** A chapter beyond the reader's position is refused (409 `chapter_not_reached`) on
read and write. Catalog: completed → last, else the monotonic `MaxChapterNumber`. Uploads keep no
high-water mark, so the frontier is the progress chapter (reflow slug, or PDF `page:N` → the chapter
whose range starts at or before it), floored by the highest chapter already reviewed — re-reading
chapter 2 must not lock out re-running chapter 7. Threads and word sentences from later chapters are
withheld.

**Storage.** The review is `book_insight.review_json` (jsonb) on the chapter's existing insight row —
same key, same replace semantics — and `Text` holds a server-rendered Markdown fallback, so every
existing reader of insights shows it unchanged. `save_insight` over a reviewed row is now 409
`review_exists` rather than silently dropping the structure. Questions live in `review_question`
(cascade from the insight), synced by prompt hash on re-save: an unchanged question keeps its SRS
state. `GET /me/review-questions/due` and `POST …/{id}/answer` (forgot/almost/knew → `SrsEngine`)
are the queue. Guests may use all of it — it costs us no inference.

**Also.** The `insights` rate limit is now partitioned by user, not IP: every MCP write arrives from
the bridge's one container address, so 60/min was a budget shared by every MCP user. The review tools
return their JSON unescaped — the default encoder writes Cyrillic and em dashes as `\uXXXX`, six
characters per letter in the model's context.

**Found while scoping, not fixed here.** The owner's DDIA is a PDF whose 8 "chapters" are the book's
Parts: PDF chapter detection reads outline level 1 only. A review there is a whole Part in recall
mode. Outline-level-2 extraction + re-extract is a follow-up slice.

<a id="2026-09-29-mcp-chatgpt-connects-with-a-personal-url"></a>

## MCP — ChatGPT connects with a personal URL — backend, web, mobile, infra — 2026-09-29

ChatGPT could add TextStack as a connector and see the public catalog, but never the reader's own
books, highlights or words. Its connector settings offer "No authentication" or OAuth and nothing
that sends `Authorization: Bearer`, and the MCP host read the key from that header only
(`HttpContextTokenProvider`). So every user-scoped tool answered "authentication required".

**What changed.** A connect key can now travel in the path: `https://textstack.app/mcp/k/tsk_…`.
The MCP host rewrites that to `/mcp` with the key as a bearer *before routing*
(`Auth/ConnectUrl.cs`), so everything after it is the existing path — the API still resolves the
key and still revokes it on the next request, with no cache. No new entity, no migration: the URL
is a second format of the same key, shown once next to the Claude Desktop config on the web connect
page and the mobile *Connect assistant* screen.

**The URL is a password, so it has to stay out of logs.** Three places would have written it:

- ASP.NET's hosting logger records "Request starting POST …/mcp/k/tsk_…" at Information, *before*
  any middleware runs — so the rewrite alone doesn't help. The host now logs that category and
  Routing at Warning.
- nginx's default combined format logs the full request line. `location /mcp/k/` has
  `access_log off`, and `error_log … crit`, because a `limit_req` rejection logs the request too.
- Cloudflare sees it regardless. Accepted until OAuth.

**One trap worth recording.** Minimal hosting inserts `UseRouting` at the *start* of the pipeline
unless you call it yourself, so a path rewrite in `app.Use` runs after the endpoint was already
chosen and does nothing. The host now calls `app.UseRouting()` explicitly after the rewrite. The
over-the-wire test (`ConnectUrl_NoHeader_KeyInPath_ForwardedAsBearer`) fails without that line —
checked by removing it.

**Verified:** unit tests for the rewrite (valid, trailing slash, explicit header wins, malformed →
404, other paths untouched); over-the-wire with the real MCP client connecting by URL; and on a
rebuilt local `mcp-server` container: live key → `get_my_reading` answers, revoked key →
"authentication required", `grep tsk_` over the container logs → 0.

**Not verified:** ChatGPT itself. Whether Developer mode is available on the owner's plan, and what
it asks before a write, is an owner step; the answer goes into `docs/05-features/mcp.md`.
<a id="2026-09-28-seo-indexnow-bing-refuses-separately"></a>

## SEO — Bing refuses IndexNow for its own reason, and the log now says which — web — 2026-09-28

With the key file fixed and deployed, the next rebuild still logged `IndexNow: 2028 URLs → 403`.
Submitting one URL by hand and reading the **body** told the two failures apart:

- `yandex.com/indexnow` → `202 {"success":true}` — the key file is right.
- `api.indexnow.org` and `bing.com/indexnow` → `403 {"errorCode":"UserForbiddedToAccessSite"}`.

`api.indexnow.org` returns Bing's verdict, and the protocol shares a URL with the other engines only
after one accepts it — so while Bing refused, Yandex heard nothing either. Sharing runs the other way
too: the one URL Yandex accepted by hand appeared in Bing Webmaster Tools' IndexNow report within the
hour (source "Self", crawl pending). Bing refuses the site's direct submissions but takes them via
Yandex. And a log line of bare status codes made "wrong key file" and "Bing refuses the site" look
identical.

**Changes.** The worker logs the response body on failure, per endpoint. It submits to `yandex.com`
directly as well, so Yandex no longer depends on Bing accepting — and through sharing, Bing receives
the URLs too.

Bing's direct refusal is not fixed by code, and it is not verification: textstack.app is verified in
Bing Webmaster Tools.

**Confirmed the same night: Bing binds a site to the first key it verified.** With the Feb–Apr key file
(`0c418872…`) served again next to the current one, one URL each:

| | key `0c418872…` (Feb–Apr) | key `a2157831…` |
|---|---|---|
| `bing.com/indexnow` | **200** | 403 `UserForbiddedToAccessSite` |
| `api.indexnow.org` | **200** | 403 |
| `yandex.com/indexnow` | 202 | 202 |

So the protocol's "any key works as long as the file matches" holds for Yandex and not for Bing. Prod's
`INDEXNOW_KEY` is back on `0c418872…` (manual `.env` edit + `--force-recreate` of `ssg-worker`). Both key
files stay served. The lesson for next time: **an IndexNow key is not rotatable on Bing** — keep the
first one, and never let the env var and the file drift, which the worker now refuses to do silently.

<a id="2026-09-28-seo-indexnow-refused-on-every-rebuild"></a>

## SEO — IndexNow had been refused (403) on every rebuild since late April — web — 2026-09-28

Every SSG rebuild ended with `IndexNow: 2028 URLs → 403` in the worker log, and nothing read it.

IndexNow proves you own a host by fetching `https://<host>/<key>.txt` and checking that it contains
the key you submitted. The repo has shipped a key file since 2026-02-04 (`da995a00`), and for a while
it worked: Bing Webmaster Tools' IndexNow report shows 31.7K URLs received between 1 and 21 April 2026,
the last batch on 22 April — then nothing. By September prod's `.env` held a different `INDEXNOW_KEY`
(`a2157831…`) from the committed file (`0c418872…`). When and why it changed isn't recorded; 22 April
is the likeliest date. The worker submitted `a2157831…`, IndexNow fetched `/a2157831….txt`, and nginx,
finding no such file, answered with the SPA's `index.html`: `200 text/html`, a page that does not
contain the key. So: 403, every rebuild, for five months, and no engine heard about a page this way.

It was invisible for the usual reason here — the failure is a log line in a job that still reports
`Completed`, because a failed submission is deliberately not allowed to fail the build.

**Fix.** The key file is renamed to the key prod actually uses (the protocol registers no key, so any
key should work as long as the file matches it — true for Yandex; Bing may disagree, see the
follow-up — and changing the file avoids a manual edit of prod's `.env`). And the worker now checks, before submitting, that `dist/<key>.txt` exists and holds
the key — if not, it logs that the two disagree and skips, instead of spending the request on a 403.

**Verify after deploy:** `curl https://textstack.app/a2157831e439403b1d4f063fb13f27a0.txt` returns the
key as `text/plain`, and the next rebuild logs `IndexNow: … → 200` (or `202`).

**Update, same night:** the key file half is fixed — Yandex now answers `202`. Bing still answers 403,
for a different reason; see [the follow-up](#2026-09-28-seo-indexnow-bing-refuses-separately).

<a id="2026-09-28-offline-a-downloaded-book-stops-waiting-for-the-network"></a>

## Offline — a downloaded book stops waiting for the network — mobile — 2026-09-28

Both chapter loaders asked the server first and fell back to SQLite when the request failed. That
reads as the safe order, and on a plane it is: `fetch` rejects on DNS or connect in milliseconds, so
the cache answers almost immediately. It is wrong on every network that is **present but useless** —
a captive portal, a tunnel, hotel Wi-Fi that accepts the connection and then says nothing. There the
reader watched a spinner for the whole socket timeout with the entire book already on the phone,
which is the precise opposite of the promise the automatic download had just made.

The order is now device first. When the cache answers, the request still goes out, and its only job is
to refresh the stored row for the *next* open — `refreshCachedChapter` is an `UPDATE` rather than the
`INSERT OR REPLACE` used at download time, deliberately: it must not create a row (only a download
decides what this device keeps) and must not restamp `cached_at`, which `listCachedChapters` uses as
its ordering key for want of a chapter number. Re-caching with the insert would have sent every
chapter the reader visited to the bottom of the offline table of contents. The rendered chapter is
never swapped, either, because that would re-run position restoration on a page someone is reading.

Two things surfaced while doing it. The catalogue chapter cache had **no `chapter_id` column**, and the
offline path filled the field with an empty string — so highlights, bookmarks, vocabulary and the
server progress write, all keyed by chapter id, had silently been unavailable for a downloaded
catalogue book. That column exists now (one `ALTER TABLE` in a `try`, which is how SQLite does
`ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`), old rows keep a NULL and stay readable. A row without an id is the one
case where the fresh copy does replace what is on screen — it can be read but not written to, and
highlights do not even load without an id — which costs one re-render, once, because the same request
gives the row its id for every later open. And `deleteOriginal` never pruned
the LRU index it shares with `evictToBudget`, so every hand-removed download left a timestamp behind
for the life of the install.

**Infinite scroll was worse than the initial load and nobody had noticed.** The catalogue's
`useReaderInfiniteScroll` read no cache at all — it was network-only — and its failure branch is
`disableInfiniteScroll()`. So offline, a fully downloaded catalogue book scrolled to the bottom of its
first chapter and then quietly stopped scrolling: no error, nothing to retry, and the reader left to
work out that chapter navigation still worked. The uploads path had carried a fallback since it was
written; the catalogue one was simply missed. Both are device-first now, for the same reason as the
initial load and with more at stake, because here a hanging socket does not just delay the reader — it
switches the feature off mid-book.

**Deleted elsewhere, deleted here.** A book removed from another phone or from the web used to keep its
copy on this device: still on the shelf, still readable, still spending a budget meant for books the
reader has. The automatic sweep now removes those, and the safety is entirely in *where* it runs — it
is the one place that has just asked the server what the library is, and `Promise.all` rejects on a
failed listing, so an empty answer means empty rather than unreachable. `chooseOrphanedDownloads` is
pure and tested against exactly that: an empty account wipes, because the caller guarantees the
listing succeeded.

Recorded in `CLAUDE.md` as a rule rather than left as a property, along with what an account is still
for now that reading needs none of it: cross-device sync, the upload quota, and wiping one account's
private files at sign-out — without which a reader's PDF stays on the phone for whoever signs in next.

<a id="2026-09-28-offline-the-door-and-the-book-that-was-not-ready"></a>

## Offline — the door, and the book that was not ready yet — mobile — 2026-09-28

Two gaps in the offline-by-default work, both found by running it on a device, neither visible to any
check in CI.

**The "+" still asked a fresh install to sign in.** `UploadTabButton` read `canUpload` — which is
`hasSession` — found none, and pushed `/(auth)/login` *before* reaching `/my-books/upload`, the route
that mints a guest on arrival. So the wall that was reported closed three merges earlier was still
standing one step further out. What makes this worth writing down is that the rule was already
written, in `app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx`, in as many words — *"`canUpload` is the wrong question for a
door"* — and applied to the tab's visibility while the tab's own handler went on asking it.
`uploadDoorIsNotGated.test.ts` now asserts it of both, and was checked by putting the bug back.

**A book you had just uploaded sat there saying "Download".** The sweep the upload screen asks for
runs while the book is still `Processing` — extraction happens in the Worker — and
`chooseAutoDownloads` takes `Ready` books only, correctly. Nothing ran again until the reader
backgrounded the app or the network changed. Upload a book, stay where you are, and your own book is
in the cloud.

The first attempt at this fix was wrong and the device said so. It went in the library screen, which
already polls while anything is processing — but that poll only exists while that screen is mounted,
so it worked or not depending on which tab the reader happened to be looking at. The test that
"passed" did so because the book had gone `Ready` while the reader was on Discover.

It belongs where the sweep lives. `DownloadContext` now notices that the listing it just read
contains a book still being processed, and schedules one re-check twelve seconds out — bounded by a
real condition rather than a clock: it stops the moment nothing is processing, with a ceiling of 25
tries for a book whose ingestion wedges forever. Verified on a Pixel 7 Pro against production, upload
to shelf without leaving the app: sweep at 13:57:55 found nothing to fetch, the re-check at 13:58:07
found the book ready, and the original and its chapter were on the device a second later.

<a id="2026-09-28-advisories-a-check-that-can-say-it-failed"></a>

## CI — a check that can say it failed — infra — 2026-09-28

The `advisories` job went red on every pull request after the Expo SDK 57 upgrade, and its log was
empty. `pnpm audit` on that dependency tree produced no report and never returned; CI killed the job
at its ten-minute ceiling.

**The bug worth fixing was not pnpm's.** A red cross that means *we did not check* is
indistinguishable from one that means *we found something*, and the check had no way to tell them
apart — it had two outcomes where it needed three. It also had a hatch that made the confusion
symmetrical: if every known advisory vanished at once, it decided the lookup was broken and exited
**zero**. The reasoning was sound (three upstreams do not ship fixes in the same minute) and the
conclusion was backwards. A lookup that cannot be trusted is exactly when a check must say so, not
when it should wave the tree through.

So the outcomes are three now — clean, an advisory nobody wrote down, and **COULD NOT CHECK**, which
fails with its own words and says in as many that it is not a verdict about the dependency tree.

**And the dependency on a package manager is gone.** `pnpm audit` only ever asked the npm registry;
the check asks it directly. It reads `pnpm-lock.yaml` — 1,046 entries, 906 names — and posts them to
the same bulk advisory endpoint, which filters by version itself, so there is no semver matching here
to get wrong. The lane in CI lost its `pnpm/action-setup` step: the job now has no package manager in
it at all, which is the point. Under a second, against ten minutes of nothing.

Line-scanning rather than YAML parsing, in the manner of `catalogBump.mjs` and
`check-node-version.mjs` next door: this workspace has no YAML parser, the root `package.json` has no
dependencies whatsoever, and adding one to read a machine-generated file is a poor trade. The one
assumption — that inside `packages:` every key is `name@version`, peer suffixes living only in
`snapshots:` — is pinned by a test, and when it stops holding the extraction yields nothing and the
caller reports COULD NOT CHECK, which is the safe direction to fail in.

**What this deliberately did not do is chase a pnpm version.** 11.27.1 fixes `pnpm audit` and then
stops the `e2e` job from exiting, twice out of two runs, eighteen minutes after its tests pass.
12.6.0 fixes the audit and [breaks catalog resolution](https://github.com/pnpm/pnpm/issues/10594).
Picking among those is trading one hang for another and calling it progress; the pin stays where it
is, and now it does not matter, because nothing in the security lane runs pnpm.

One more hole closed on the way, found by accident while testing the others: `strayLockfiles()` threw
an uncaught error when git could not answer. "git did not reply" is not "there are no stray
lockfiles" — the same mistake, one function over. It reports COULD NOT CHECK too.

<a id="2026-09-28-mobile-expo-55-to-57"></a>

## Mobile — Expo 55 → 57, in one step — mobile — 2026-09-28

The plan said SDK 56. By the time it came up, 57 was `latest`, and the check that settled it was
comparing what each SDK actually pulls in: **56 and 57 bundle identical third-party native modules**
— async-storage 2.2.0, netinfo 12.0.1, safe-area-context 5.7, webview 13.16.1, screens 4.26. The only
difference between them is React Native (0.85.3 against 0.86.3) and Expo's own packages. Stopping at
56 would have bought a second upgrade in six months and reduced no risk, which on a twice-yearly
cadence means being a version behind on the day the work is done.

React Native 0.83.10 → 0.86.3 broke two things, both small and both real:

- `StyleSheet.absoluteFillObject` is gone. `absoluteFill` is now the plain object that
  `absoluteFillObject` used to be, so the six call sites are a rename with identical semantics.
- The tab bar hands its icon renderer a `ColorValue` rather than a `string` — it can be a
  platform-opaque token now (`PlatformColor`, `DynamicColorIOS`). `AnimatedTabIcon` takes the wider
  type; Ionicons already accepted it.

Two things `expo-doctor` found that had nothing to do with the SDK jump. `app.json` still carried a
top-level `splash` key, which SDK 57 no longer accepts — it moved into the `expo-splash-screen`
plugin, where the same three options live. And the lockfile held `@expo/dom-webview@55.0.6` as an
auto-installed peer that no `pnpm up` would move, while `@expo/log-box@57.0.4` asks for `^57.0.1`;
rewriting the resolution and reinstalling settled it. 21/21 doctor checks pass.

**TypeScript is deliberately left behind.** The SDK asks for `~6.0.3` against the workspace catalog's
5.9, and TypeScript is one version for web, admin, packages and mobile together — a major there is its
own change with its own blast radius, not a passenger on an Expo bump. It is listed in
`expo.install.exclude` so the disagreement is recorded rather than re-discovered.

React moved 19.2.0 → 19.2.3 across the **whole** workspace, because there is one React here on
purpose: hoisting can only place one copy at the root, and a mobile pin beside a different web pin is
how the web suite once failed 26 tests with "Invalid hook call". 19.2.3 is Expo's pin for SDK 57 and a
patch bump for the web.

**A device pass found what none of the above could.** `:expo-updates:kspReleaseKotlin` dies with a bare
`Metaspace` failure partway through a release build, because Expo's template writes
`-XX:MaxMetaspaceSize=512m` and SDK 57 needs more. Nothing in CI can see it: the Metro bundle,
`expo export` and every unit test pass without ever starting Gradle. And it is not a small-laptop
problem — `MaxMetaspaceSize` is a hard cap on the JVM regardless of machine memory, so an EAS worker
would stop at the same task. `plugins/with-gradle-metaspace.js` raises it, in a plugin because
`android/gradle.properties` is generated: `expo prebuild` overwrites it, and a hand-edited copy also
poisons the runtime fingerprint. Verified by deleting `android/`, prebuilding clean and building from
the plugin alone.

On the emulator afterwards, against production: the app launches, the reader renders a chapter, a
catalogue book downloads, and in airplane mode after a cold start the shelf still says "On this
device" and the chapter opens from SQLite.

**And one left broken on purpose, which is worth saying plainly.** On the SDK 57 tree, `pnpm audit` —
on the pinned pnpm 11.8.0 — produces no report at all and never exits, so the `advisories` job burns
its ten-minute ceiling and says nothing. That is the worst shape a check can fail in: it reports
nothing about whether the tree is safe and looks identical to one that found something.

pnpm 11.27.1 does exit and does report, and the report is the same two known advisories. But on that
version the **`e2e` job** stops exiting instead — twice out of two runs, hanging for eighteen minutes
after its seven tests have passed and printed "teardown complete". `e2e` is a required check and
`advisories` is not, so bumping pnpm trades a broken security check for a broken merge gate. That is
not a fix. The pin stays at 11.8.0 and the gap is recorded here. Finding a version where both commands
exit needs a bisect, and `packageManager` makes every step of it a CI round, because any other pnpm
refuses to run at all (`ERR_PNPM_BAD_PM_VERSION`).

Two corrections to how this was diagnosed, because both cost time and neither was obvious. The
hand-patched lockfile from earlier in this work — the one that unstuck `@expo/dom-webview` — looked
like the culprit and was not: a full re-resolution from an empty `node_modules` behaves identically.
And on *that* patched tree pnpm did write a complete report before wedging, which made a
harness around the timeout look like a fix; on the re-resolved tree it writes nothing through a pipe,
through a file, or on any signal, so there was never a report to rescue. The harness was written and
then deleted.

A second thing fell out of regenerating the lockfile, and it is the argument for tildes in the
catalog: a fresh resolution took `@types/react@19.3.0` — published since the pin was written — and
`expo install --check` refuses it, because SDK 57 pins the React types to React's own line (`~19.2.4`)
and React here is 19.2.3. Types from the 19.3 line describe a React this repo does not have. The
catalog entries for `@types/react` and `@types/react-dom` are tildes now. The caret had been harmless
only for as long as no new minor existed.

**The runtime fingerprint moved** — `7e62528d…` → `1150c870…` — so this cannot reach anyone over the
air. `mobile-ota.yml` already handles that case by building and submitting instead of publishing an
update, which is exactly what should happen here.

<a id="2026-09-28-guests-a-render-is-not-a-commitment"></a>

## Guests — a render is not a commitment — web — 2026-09-28

Production held **7,263 guest accounts**. 116 of them had ever done anything: saved a word, made a
highlight, uploaded a book, set a bookmark. The other 7,147 were artefacts of a page being rendered.

`ReaderPage` minted a guest on mount. The line was added on 2026-04-19 for a real reason — the first
word tap raced `ensureSession`, and the resulting flip of `isAuthenticated` re-ran the chapter fetch
and dropped the popup. A week later that race was fixed where it actually lived: `useReaderChapter`
skips a refetch whose key it has already loaded. The pre-warm has been redundant since 2026-04-26 and
nobody noticed, because what it cost was invisible from inside the app.

What it cost: every client that executes JavaScript on a chapter URL received a real `User` row with
a token pair. On a public catalogue that is mostly crawlers — and the undeclared ones, which rotate
desktop-Chrome user agents (148/149/150, Windows and Mac, no mobile and no Firefox worth the name) and
so are served the SPA rather than the prerendered page. 148 mints in two days of access logs, across
101 distinct books, each one a full reader session: `auth/me` 401, chapter fetch, `POST /auth/guest`,
then `PUT /me/progress`. The declared bots — SERanking, Semrush, YouBot, Sentry's uptime monitor —
are detected by nginx, get the SSG tree, and mint nothing.

**The progress write is what made them permanent.** `GuestCleanupWorker` deletes inactive guests only
when they hold nothing durable, and it counts a `ReadingProgress` row as durable — correctly, because
that is somebody's place in a book. So 5,600 rows that existed for under five seconds were going to
live forever, along with 5,746 progress rows, 4,110 library rows and 7,228 refresh tokens.

The mint is now back where [ADR-014](../01-architecture/adr/ADR-014-guest-sessions.md) put it: a
commitment signal — on this page, the first word tap, which calls `ensureSession` itself. Until then
an anonymous reader's progress goes to localStorage, and `flushLocalProgress` delivers it the moment
a session exists, so nothing is lost by waiting. `readerDoesNotMintOnMount.test.ts` fails the build if
the pre-warm returns, because it reads as an obvious performance nicety and that is precisely how it
got in.

Nothing here is a bug in the crawlers. They followed links we published — `?direct=1` in the referrer
is our own table-of-contents href — and got exactly what the page offered them.

<a id="2026-09-28-uploads-the-button-gives-back-your-file"></a>

## Uploads — the button gives back your file, not our copy of it — mobile — 2026-09-28

Two buttons sat side by side on an uploaded book. One said **Download for Offline** and put the
reader's own file on the device, byte for byte, images and all. The other said **Download EPUB** and
returned a freshly built EPUB of the *extracted text* — no images at all, because `ExportUserBookAsync`
never populated them for an EPUB upload, and for a PDF upload there was nothing to populate: ADR-012
dropped inline image extraction on the reasoning that "the PDF renders its own images", which is true
only while the PDF is the thing being rendered.

So of the two downloads, the one named more plainly gave back the worse book. And it gave back nothing
at all on mobile: the button called `Linking.openURL` on an owner-scoped route, which opens the system
browser — a separate process holding none of the app's credentials — so every tap in the app's life
ended on a 401. The in-process fetch that fixed the 401 shipped earlier this month; this change
retires the thing it was fetching.

The button now shares **the original**, and when the book is already downloaded it **never touches the
network**: the file is on the device, and asking the server for another copy of it is the exact round
trip the offline work exists to remove. The only local work is a copy into the cache directory, and
only for the name — the stored original is `<bookId>.pdf`, and a share sheet shows the file name to
whatever app receives it. `bookFileName` (was `epubFileName`) now takes the extension.

**Deleted, not deprecated.** `GET /me/books/{id}/export/epub`, the anonymous
`GET /books/{slug}/export/epub` — unreachable from any UI since 2026-04-15 — `EpubExportService`,
`ExportEndpoints`, the mobile `exportEpub.ts`, a dead `bookDetail.downloadEpub` string in the web
locale, and with them the whole `TextStack.Epub` library: an EPUB *builder* that existed for one
caller, plus its tests. 15 lines of solution file went too.

Two pieces of documentation were lying and are now fixed. `CLAUDE.md` promised "EPUB export of user
highlights and notes" and a route `GET /me/export/epub`. Neither ever existed: both real routes
exported the book's **chapters**, and a unit test said so in its own name — *"export walks the book's
chapters and never reads the Highlights set"*. The same file still claimed a PDF upload's original is
not cached offline, which stopped being true yesterday.

What replaced the export is not an export. A reader who uploads a book to us already has it; the useful
thing an app can do is hand it back unchanged, and do it without a network when the bytes are already
on the phone.

<a id="2026-09-28-offline-the-shelf-says-where-each-book-is"></a>

## Offline — the shelf says where each book is — mobile — 2026-09-28

The library downloads itself now, and nothing on screen admitted it. A reader could not tell what was
on the phone, what was arriving, and what still needed a connection — and the automation alone did
not answer the objection that started this work. It removed the need to press Download; it did not
move anything the reader could see. The state was still three taps inside the book.

Every row now carries its own state and the tap that changes it. `in-cloud` renders an **action, not
a label**, and renders nothing at all when the row cannot start one: saying "this book is not here"
is only worth the pixels if the next thing the reader can do is fix it.

The order of checks in `offlineState.ts` is the design, not an implementation detail. A running
download speaks over anything cached, because it is the only truth about to change. Then completeness
— which for a PDF upload includes the original file, because chapters alone are the text and not the
book, and calling that "on this device" would re-promise exactly the substitution the reader was told
had gone. Then partial, so a stopped download reads as unfinished rather than absent: "in the cloud"
about a half-downloaded book is a lie the next flight exposes.

Both halves of the shelf, deliberately. Uploads and catalogue editions differ in one place only —
which payload the download needs and where it comes from — and a reader looking at a shelf should not
be able to tell which half they are looking at. The catalogue's detail is fetched **on tap** rather
than held for every row: a hundred editions would otherwise make a hundred requests to answer a
question only the tapped row asks.

One thing was nearly fudged and is worth recording. Whether a PDF's original is on the device is
asked of the filesystem once for the whole shelf, not derived from the `isPdf` flag on the cache row.
That flag says the book **has** an original; it never says we hold it. Reading it the other way is
reasoning in a circle, and it would have put "On this device" under a book that opens offline as text
with the figures stripped.

### The door that created the session behind it

Two places carried the same circular reasoning, and a device run found both.

The "+" tab button was gated on `canUpload`, which is `hasSession` — so an install that had never
opened a book did not see the entrance, and the entrance is what creates the session it was being
judged on. Since the upload route began minting a guest on arrival, the only install this hid the
button from is precisely the one it now works for. `canUpload` remains the right question for
anything that acts on the server; it is the wrong question for a door.

And the signed-out library offered exactly one way out: Sign In — the one thing never required to add
a book. An empty shelf has two honest answers and only the second was on screen. The primary action
is now "Upload a book", with signing in kept, quieter, and described as what it actually buys:
carrying a library to another phone. `EmptyState` gained a secondary action for that, since forcing
the choice into one button is what produced the problem.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/lib/offlineState.ts` (+ tests),
`src/components/library/OfflineStateBadge.tsx`, `src/components/library/BookList.tsx`,
`app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/library.tsx`, `src/components/ui/EmptyState.tsx`.

<a id="2026-09-28-offline-the-library-fetches-itself"></a>

## Offline — the library fetches itself — mobile — 2026-09-28

Offline was a feature you had to find: walk into a book, press "Download for Offline". Someone who
uploaded a book to us has already said they want it, and asking again is asking twice. Their own
books now arrive on their own.

Four rules, each a decision rather than an implementation detail. **Wi-Fi only** — a library of 80 MB
uploads over a metered connection is a bill nobody agreed to, and manual download still works
anywhere because it asks first. **It never evicts** — the budget stops the queue, it does not make
room; deleting a book the reader chose to keep in order to fit one they never asked for would be the
app arguing with them. **Their own uploads only** — the catalogue is 1,498 editions and stays a
deliberate tap. **One at a time**, awaited in turn, so a manual download the reader is watching never
races a background one.

Order is last-read, then newest upload. A reader on a new phone wants the book they were in the
middle of; among books never opened, the newest is the one they were most recently thinking about.
Ordering by upload date alone would start a twenty-book library with the one abandoned two years ago.

The queue runs on the two moments that change the answer — a session appearing, a network appearing —
plus an explicit call when an upload lands, because "the reader just added a book" is not an event
anything can subscribe to.

### The bug that only a device could show

The first build did nothing at all, silently. No error, no log, no download — and every participant
behaving correctly.

The sweep lived in an effect that depended on `runAutoDownload`, a `useCallback` over
`startUserBookDownload`, itself over three more. Any of those changing identity re-ran the effect,
whose cleanup cancelled the sweep in flight — while the re-entry returned immediately, because a
sweep *was* still running. The queue never reached its first book. Nothing logged, because nothing
failed.

It took a device and a production database query to see: the book sat `Ready` on the server, the
emulator was on unmetered Wi-Fi, and the cache directory was never created. Cancellation now keys on
the session rather than on the render — the sweep stops when the provider unmounts or the reader
signs out, which are the only two times stopping it is right — and one line reports the queue length
on the happy path, so "it ran and found nothing" stays distinguishable from "it never ran".

### Found while verifying, not fixed here

The same run turned up something larger and recorded for its own slice: **the app mints a new guest
instead of keeping its own.** Five guest users appeared on production from one emulator in two hours,
and the uploaded book belongs to the first of them — the others are empty libraries. It coincided with
a deploy window where the API returned 502, which fits: a token needing refresh meets a 5xx,
`onUnauthorized` correctly declines to wipe anything and returns null, `AuthContext` leaves `user`
null, and `decideMint` reads that as "no session". Server unavailability is being treated as absence
of a session. That matters more than this feature: the whole premise here is that a library belongs
to a person and follows them, and a session that silently changes on a network failure breaks it at
the root.

**Verified**: 422 mobile unit tests (11 new on the ordering and the Wi-Fi/budget gate), `tsc`, Metro
bundle, and on a Pixel 7 Pro that the sweep now runs and computes its queue. **Not verified on a
device**: a book actually arriving by itself — blocked by the guest bug above, which leaves the
current session with an empty library.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/lib/autoDownloadPolicy.ts` (+ tests),
`src/context/DownloadContext.tsx`, `app/my-books/upload.tsx`.
<a id="2026-09-28-ci-a-deploy-that-re-proved-the-pull-request"></a>

## CI — a deploy that re-proved the pull request — infra — 2026-09-28

Every merge paid for the whole suite twice. `deploy.yml` opened with
`uses: ./.github/workflows/ci.yml` — all nine jobs, the same nine the pull request had just passed —
and only then shipped. The server rebuilt the images itself on arrival (`up -d --build`), so even the
`docker` job was built twice over. A deploy took about thirty minutes, most of it re-proving what was
already proved. Three of those happened in one evening.

What the second run genuinely adds is narrow, and worth naming rather than assuming. Branch
protection here is `strict: false`: a pull request may have been tested against a base older than
main's tip, so nothing has verified the **merged** tree. The insurance worth buying is "does it still
compile and pass its tests" — `backend` and `frontend`.

`ci.yml` now takes a `scope` input. A pull request gets `full` and is unchanged; `deploy.yml` passes
`merged-tree` and gets those two. The rest is skipped for reasons rather than for speed: `mobile`,
`mobile-runtime` and `extension` do not affect what is deployed; `docker` repeats what the server
does on arrival; `node-version` and `advisories` ask questions whose answer cannot change by merging.

**`e2e` is the deliberate omission and the only one with a real cost.** A regression that only it
catches would reach production without it having run on the exact merged tree. It ran on the pull
request's head, and with one merge at a time the difference between the two is the previous merge.
Stated here rather than discovered later — and if it ever catches something in the gap, the fix is to
put it back, not to argue with the evidence.

**Files**: `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.github/workflows/deploy.yml`.

<a id="2026-09-28-auth-a-session-restored-and-immediately-replaced"></a>

## Auth — a session restored and immediately replaced — mobile — 2026-09-28

Five guest users appeared on production from one device in two hours. The first owned the uploaded
book; the rest were empty libraries. From inside the app this is invisible and reads as "my books
disappeared".

**The cause is an ordering contract, and no network failure is involved.** `userRef.current = user`
is assigned during render. `AuthContext`'s bootstrap calls `setUser(restored)` and then, in the same
tick, `sessionReadyRef.resolve()` — which releases everything parked in `waitForSession()`. Those
continuations resume as microtasks, **before** React commits. `decideMint` reads the ref, sees `null`
for a reader with a perfectly good stored session, and mints a fresh guest over the top of it. The
previous guest's library stays on the server, unreachable, and `GuestCleanupWorker` keeps it forever
because it holds an upload.

The fix is one line: write the ref synchronously beside `setUser`.

**A correction to what was published yesterday.** The entry for the auto-download slice, and the pull
request that carried it, both attributed this to the 502 deploy window — a token refresh meeting a
5xx, `onUnauthorized` correctly declining to wipe, `user` left null. That theory was wrong and was
recorded before it was checked. `onUnauthorized` does handle 5xx correctly, and the guest mint does
persist `user` to SecureStore; neither is the problem. The race is entirely local and would happen on
a perfect network. The timing coincidence with the deploy was exactly that.

`authBootstrapOrder.test.ts` asserts the ordering against the source, in the style of
`capabilityLiterals.test.ts`, because the line it protects looks redundant — `userRef.current = user`
sits a few lines above it — and is precisely what a later tidy-up removes. Verified by deleting the
fix and watching the test fail.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/context/AuthContext.tsx`, `src/lib/authBootstrapOrder.test.ts`.

<a id="2026-09-28-uploads-a-sign-up-wall-that-only-existed-because-nothing-had-asked"></a>

## Uploads — a sign-up wall that only existed because nothing had asked — mobile — 2026-09-28

An install that had never opened a book, tapping Upload, was told: *"Your books need somewhere to
live. Create a free account."* Everything about that was internally consistent and the conclusion was
still wrong. `canUpload` is `hasSession`; mobile minted a guest only in `ReaderSessionGate`, which
wraps the two reader routes; so a reader who came to upload first had no session, and the screen
offered the one thing that would create one.

The comment defending it had gone circular — *"there is genuinely no row to attach a file to … the
honest and only actionable next step is an account"*. There was no row because nothing on that path
had asked for one, and a guest is explicitly allowed to upload (ADR-014 §3a, since 2026-09-06).
**Choosing to upload a book is at least as strong a commitment signal as opening one**, which is the
signal the reader gate was built around in the first place.

So the upload route is wrapped in the same gate, which is now called `SessionGate` rather than
`ReaderSessionGate` because it guards two different commitments. Nothing else about it changed: same
3s deadline, same single-flighted `ensureSession`, same rule that every failure still renders the
thing behind it. The account screen survives for the case where minting genuinely fails — offline,
rate limited, a wedged bootstrap — where an account really is the only actionable step, and saying so
is honest rather than a default.

Found on a device, and confirmed on one. On a fresh install with the app's data cleared, deep-linking
to `/my-books/upload` used to show the account wall; it now shows the upload screen with the guest
tier's `0 B / 50.0 MB used`. Worth noting what the same run showed about how a new reader reaches
that screen at all: in the first-run Discover layout there is no "+" button, and the Library tab
