offers "Sign In" — so the upload route is currently hard to reach without an account even though
uploading no longer needs one. That is a separate piece of work.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/components/SessionGate.tsx` (moved from `components/reader/`),
`app/my-books/upload.tsx`, the two reader routes, `src/lib/capabilities.ts`.

<a id="2026-09-28-offline-a-budget-for-the-phone-and-two-bugs-only-a-device-could-show"></a>

## Offline — a budget for the phone, and two bugs only a device could show — mobile — 2026-09-28

Nothing on the device had any storage accounting at all: no limit, no TTL, no eviction, no check
before a download. That was survivable while every download was an explicit tap that fetched chapter
text. It stopped being survivable the moment a download started including a 20–80 MB file, and it
would be irresponsible once the library begins downloading itself.

The budget is **2 GB**, level with the largest real tier, so a reader whose server-side library fits
their plan also fits on their phone and eviction is for the tail rather than the norm. Order is
least-recently-**opened**, tracked in AsyncStorage — the modern `expo-file-system` cannot touch an
mtime (the trick `EdgeTtsService` uses server-side), and a file's own timestamp is when it was
*downloaded*, so evicting by it throws away the book someone reads daily in favour of one grabbed
yesterday and never opened. A column on the SQLite meta was the other option and is worse: that
schema is `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` with no migration path, so a new column silently never
appears on an install that already has the table.

`chooseEvictions` is pure and tested, including the case that matters most: it returns a set that
does **not** reach the budget rather than evict a protected book. Better to sit over budget than to
delete a file under a reader. That is cheaper than it sounds — evicting costs bytes only, because
the reading position lives in `progressStorage` and on the server.

A full device is also no longer treated as a transient failure. `SQLITE_FULL` went through the same
three-retries-with-backoff as a flaky network, so a 120-chapter book spent about three minutes
failing the 110 that remained and then advised "Tap Retry", which cannot help. It now stops at the
first one, keeps what is cached, and says the device is out of space — which the web side has done
since it was written.

The speech cache was swept up on the way past. `useTts` has been filling `${cacheDirectory}tts/`
with one clip per distinct (word, language, speed) since it was written and deletes from it **only
on a failed download** — it grows for the life of the install and shrinks only when Android reclaims
the whole cache directory. It is now trimmed to 200 MB once per launch, reusing `chooseEvictions`
rather than repeating the arithmetic. And `formatBytes` existed in three copies that had already
begun to differ in their decimals; there is one now.

### What the emulator found that CI could not

The `file://` spike passed — that is the headline, and it is in the previous entry's feature. Running
the real app on a Pixel 7 Pro also turned up two things no test would have:

**Deleting a book left its downloaded original on the phone.** The library went empty and
`files/originals/<id>.pdf` was still there, verified over adb. "Remove download" and "Delete book"
are different buttons and only the first one cleaned up, so a deleted book left tens of megabytes of
the reader's own private file behind, belonging to a book that no longer exists, with nothing left in
the UI that could ever remove it.

**A fresh install hits a sign-up wall on upload.** An install that has never opened a book has no
session, `canUpload` is `hasSession`, and the upload screen therefore offers "Create a free account"
— to a reader for whom uploading is allowed (ADR-014 §3a, since 2026-09-06). Opening any book mints
the guest and the same button then shows the 50 MB guest quota and works. Recorded for the next
slice rather than fixed here: the fix is to mint on this entry point too, not to change the policy.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/lib/{originalFilePolicy,originalFileCache,deviceStorage,formatBytes}.ts`,
`src/components/library/DeviceStorageRow.tsx`, `src/context/DownloadContext.tsx`,
`app/_layout.tsx`, `app/(tabs)/profile.tsx`, `app/my-books/[id].tsx`.

<a id="2026-09-27-offline-the-book-you-downloaded-is-the-book-you-get"></a>

## Offline — the book you downloaded is the book you get — mobile, backend, shared — 2026-09-27

Offline, a PDF upload was substituted with its extracted text. The button said so before the tap,
which made it honest and did not make it good: Android is the primary product, and every reader in
the category — the local ones (ReadEra, Moon+, Librera) and the cloud libraries (Kindle, Play Books,
Kobo) alike — stores the file on the device. Page-by-page streaming is what you do when you are
deliberately *not* giving someone the file. Here the reader uploaded it themselves.

**The substitution was worse than the warning admitted.** ADR-012 dropped inline image extraction on
the reasoning that "the PDF renders its own images" — true, and only while the PDF is rendering.
Offline it was not, so what the reader got was the text with the figures removed, from books chosen
for being full of figures. The same ADR opens by arguing that "reflow is structurally unreadable for
the target table-heavy books". Offline served exactly that unreadable version of exactly those books.

And the constraint turned out not to be a decision at all. **ADR-012 never ruled on offline** — no
mention of it, of caching, or of storing the file on a device; it says the opposite, that "rendering
the original PDF needs only the stored file". "No offline form" was a property of how slice S4b built
the transport, inherited as though it were a choice. There was nothing to overturn.

So the download takes the file. `apps/mobile/src/lib/originalFileCache.ts` stores it under
`Paths.document` — not `Paths.cache`, which the OS evicts under pressure: a book someone asked to
keep is not a cache entry. It writes to a `.part` sibling and renames on success, so a killed
download cannot leave a truncated file that clears the size floor and opens as garbage. The reader
prefers a stored file whenever one exists, **online too**: it opens faster than a Range stream, and
it keeps the offline path in daily use instead of reserving it for emergencies.

pdf.js reads it same-origin, which is the whole trick: the bare filename against a `baseUrl` of the
file's own directory, with `allowFileAccess` and `allowFileAccessFromFileURLs`. Deliberately not
`allowUniversalAccessFromFileURLs`, which grants a file page every origin including http(s). Byte
transfer over the bridge was never on the table — S4b already rejected it, 21 MB puts the memory over.

What this removes is as interesting as what it adds. `offlineReflowOfPdfRef` — the suppression that
stops an offline reflow session writing a chapter-space position over a stored page — survives, but
narrows to the only case left: a book downloaded before originals existed. With the file present
there is no coordinate change to suppress, because offline and online are now the same viewer in the
same space.

**Scope note, decided by the owner and worth stating plainly:** the endpoint now serves originals of
any upload format, EPUB included, which required lifting a deliberate `Format == BookFormat.Pdf`
filter. For EPUB there is no reader-visible payoff today — nothing renders an original EPUB, and its
canonical view is the reflow. A side effect fell out of it: an HTML clip's stored source became
fetchable by its owner, as `application/octet-stream` rather than `text/html`, because user HTML
executing on the API origin is self-XSS.

### What the review caught

An adversarial pass over the branch found twelve things; two were real defects rather than polish,
and both came from the feature's own success.

**Offline resume was lost for exactly the books this adds.** The PDF resume page came only from the
server, so offline it fell to page 1. That had been survivable while an offline PDF was not opened in
Original layout at all — the reflow reader has its own local fallback — but with the file on the
device, the headline scenario (read on a plane, close, reopen) lost the place it used to keep.
`writePdfProgress` had been saving the page locally the whole time and nothing had ever read it back.

**A second tap ran two downloads into one `.part` path**, where the second one's opening `delete()`
removes the file the first is still streaming into, and the loser renames a truncated document onto
the real name. The download is now single-flighted — but the actual defect was the button:
`runDownload` flips the status to `complete` before the file starts, so the UI rendered its idle
"Download for Offline" state for the whole of a 21 MB transfer, and a reader who saw nothing happen
tapped again. The file now downloads *before* the chapters, inside the window where the status
already says downloading.

The rest were smaller and all real: the viewer was being built twice on an online open of a
downloaded book (a wasted token fetch, a wasted Range stream, resume running twice); the size was
advertised on books that never fetch a file, because the server reports the newest stored file of any
format; the offline banner still said "the downloaded text of this PDF" about a book that now opens
as itself; a Remove during an in-flight download left an orphan file; the filename guard could throw
out through an `onPress`; the metadata response defaulted the new size field to null and would have
silently turned the button's size off; `.part` files counted toward the storage figure; and a failed
original finished as "Downloaded", to be discovered on the plane — it is now recorded, surfaced and
retryable. Two comments were factually wrong and were corrected rather than deleted.

**A test fixture was lying, too.** The integration suite's `SamplePdfBytes()` had no
`startxref`/`%%EOF` tail, so `PdfUploadSanity.LooksLikeCompletePdf` rejected it, the upload helper
returned null, and five PDF tests had been skipping silently since that guard landed — including the
one asserting a PDF still serves as `application/pdf`. Fixed; they run now.

**Not verified: any of it on a device.** The one question only hardware can answer is whether pdf.js
inside the WebView actually reads the `file://` it is handed. If it does not, ADR-012's recorded
fallback is a native renderer — which costs selectable text, and therefore translation and
vocabulary, so that would be a reason to reconsider the approach rather than push through it.

**Files**: `apps/mobile/src/lib/originalFileCache.ts`, `originalFilePolicy.ts` (+ tests),
`src/components/reader/{ReaderShell,useUserBookReaderSource}.tsx`, `src/context/DownloadContext.tsx`,
`app/my-books/[id].tsx`, `src/lib/cachedUserBookDetail.ts`,
`backend/src/Api/Endpoints/UserBooksEndpoints.cs`, `backend/src/Application/UserBooks/`,
`packages/shared/src/api/userBooks.ts`.

<a id="2026-09-27-mobile-a-refusal-that-was-correct-for-twelve-days-now-ships-the-build-itself"></a>

## Mobile — a refusal that was correct for twelve days — mobile — 2026-09-27

Offline reading merged on 2026-09-14 and reached no one. It was not a bug in the feature, and not a
misconfiguration: #612 added `expo-sharing` for the EPUB share sheet — a native module with a config
plugin — and `app.json` sets `runtimeVersion: { policy: "fingerprint" }`, so the fingerprint moved
and an OTA update could no longer reach any installed build. `mobile-ota.yml` compared the two
runtimes, refused, and failed the run that same night, exactly as designed:

> `eas update` still SUCCEEDS, publishing to a runtime nobody has installed. A green check and zero
> delivery.

Every part of the machinery was right. The PR-time warning fired too — `mobile-runtime` in CI reported
the move on #612 before it merged, deliberately without failing, because *"a check that blocked them
would be turned off within a week."* And then nothing happened for twelve days, because **a failed run
is not a task list**: it scrolls out of sight, and the one step between "an update cannot reach anyone"
and "somebody starts a build" was a human remembering. The same refusal fired again today on #621.

So the refusal now does the thing it was asking for. On a fingerprint mismatch `mobile-ota.yml` starts
`eas build --profile production --auto-submit-with-profile closed --no-wait` instead of exiting 1.
`closed` — Play's `alpha` — because that is where the testers are: six opted in, while the Internal
track's list is empty, and a build sent where nobody is subscribed repeats this outage in a quieter
way. `production` stays a human click in `mobile-release.yml`, because it has an audience that did not
ask to be a test. The stated cost, accepted rather than discovered: every native change on `main` now
spends an EAS build and a `versionCode`.

A third hole, found while writing this up and closed in the same change: the workflow's own push
trigger listed `apps/mobile/**` and `packages/**`, but the fingerprint resolves through the workspace
root — the pnpm migration moved 122 of 135 fingerprint sources for precisely that reason — so
`pnpm-lock.yaml` and `pnpm-workspace.yaml` move the mobile runtime with nothing under `apps/mobile`
changing, and the workflow would not have run at all. It had been survivable while the only thing
editing those files was a person. It stopped being survivable the same day: the semiannual dependency
lane now edits both, unattended, twice a year. A lockfile bump would have moved the runtime,
`mobile-runtime` would have warned on the pull request, and the merge would have done nothing — the
same outage, arriving more slowly. The trigger paths now match that job's own diff filter.

Two other details are load-bearing and neither is obvious. The permission check runs **before** the build —
Android permissions here are governed by `blockedPermissions` with no allowlist, so a new permission
from a native module arrives silently — and the summary records that it ran at the *source* manifest
level, not the merged one, because catching library-injected permissions needs
`gradlew :app:processReleaseMainManifest`, ten minutes and an Android SDK. Then the generated
`android/` directory is **deleted again** before `eas build`: a leftover one makes EAS treat the project
as bare, skip its own prebuild and build from those local files, which moves the very fingerprint that
was measured two steps earlier. That is the poisoning `docs/03-ops/play-store-release.md` warns about
for local fingerprint measurements, with a build attached to it.

One thing the plan had wrong, found by doing it: a single build cannot be submitted to two tracks.
Play identifies a release by `versionCode`, so the second `eas submit` — build 28 to `closed` after
`internal` — failed with *"You've already submitted this version of the app."* Reaching a second track
is a **promotion in Play Console** (Add from library), which `eas submit` does not expose. Build 28 was
promoted that way — and it is the reason the automatic path targets one track with an audience rather
than trying for two. That also
explains the record for 2026-09-11, which shows submissions to both `internal` and `alpha`: those were
two different builds, 26 and 27, not one build twice.

Shipping the backlog by hand first turned up three more things the repo was wrong about. The newest finished
build is **27** (2026-09-11), not 24 — `docs/STATUS.md` was two builds behind, while
`docs/qa/scenarios/QA-006` was right all along. Builds 25–27 were made from a laptop, not through
`mobile-release.yml`, so that workflow's run history reads as one release since 2026-09-02 and is not
the record of what testers hold. And the permission guard, run as a pre-flight, reported *"37
permissions, none unsanctioned"* against a merged manifest dated **20 August** — a month and two native
dependencies stale. Regenerated and rebuilt, it says the same thing, which is now worth something:
`expo-sharing` declares no new permission.

Also corrected, because three stale claims in three files had each survived long enough to mislead:
`CLAUDE.md` said `EXPO_TOKEN` "is NOT currently set" (set since 2026-09-01) and that `eas.json` has no
`submit.production` profile (it has one, at a 10% staged rollout); `ci.yml` said `apps/mobile` is "npm
+ its own lockfile, not part of the pnpm workspace", contradicted by the `pnpm --filter mobile` steps
directly beneath it ever since #514; and `docs/05-features/offline-reading.md` described the mobile
implementation with no mention that it needed a build, opening with "added 2026-09-14" in a way that
reads as delivered.

**Files**: `.github/workflows/mobile-ota.yml`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `CLAUDE.md`,
`docs/STATUS.md`, `docs/05-features/offline-reading.md`.

<a id="2026-09-27-deps-two-package-families-that-dependabot-split-one-at-a-time"></a>

## Deps — two package families that Dependabot split one at a time — backend — 2026-09-27

Merging the backlog of per-package Dependabot pull requests left `Directory.Packages.props` holding
two families at two versions each: `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration.Abstractions` at 10.0.12 with
its six runtime siblings still on 10.0.11, and `Microsoft.Extensions.AI.Evaluation` plus `.Quality`
at 10.10.0 with `Microsoft.Extensions.AI` and `.Evaluation.Reporting` still on 10.6.0. Both families
ship as one version number from one repository.

Nothing caught it, and nothing was going to: each pull request was green on its own, which is the
whole shape of the problem — "one package per PR" cannot see a family, and a green build is not
evidence that two halves of one library agree. It was the sixth such pull request, for
`Microsoft.Extensions.DependencyInjection`, that made it visible, by failing to merge with a
conflict against the fifth.

All eleven entries are now on one version per family (10.0.12 and 10.10.0). That sixth pull request
was closed rather than rebased: it moved one package of the seven, and would have left the split
intact in a different place.

This is the failure mode `scripts/nugetBump.mjs` was given a lockstep guard for on the same day, for
`ModelContextProtocol` and its ASP.NET transport. The semiannual lane would not have produced this
split in the first place — one run raises every member of a family to the same latest in one commit —
and the per-package lane that did produce it is now off (`open-pull-requests-limit: 0`).

Verified: `dotnet restore`, `dotnet build` (0 errors), 1288 unit tests.

**Files**: `Directory.Packages.props`.

<a id="2026-09-27-deps-one-proposal-twice-a-year-and-a-vulnerability-that-does-not-wait-for-it"></a>

## Deps — one proposal twice a year, and a vulnerability that does not wait for it — infra — 2026-09-27

Six open pull requests, none merged, all dependency bumps. Five were Dependabot's, green for
thirteen days, and their entire content was Dapper plus four `Microsoft.Extensions` patches. The
sixth, `deps/catalog` from our own weekly workflow, was **unmergeable by construction**: a pull
request opened by `peter-evans/create-pull-request` under `GITHUB_TOKEN` fires no `pull_request`
event — GitHub's own loop protection — and `ci.yml` listened for nothing else. So `backend`,
`frontend`, `docker` and `e2e` never reported, branch protection saw four required contexts missing,
and the only check on the pull request was GitGuardian. It had been waiting since 21 September with
two verified bumps nobody could take.

The fix for that is three lines and no secret: `ci.yml` now also triggers on a push to `deps/**`.
Checks land on the head SHA, which is all branch protection reads. A PAT would have worked too, and
would have been a credential to rotate forever. Verified first that none of the four required jobs
needs a pull-request context — `github.event.pull_request` and `github.base_ref` appear only inside
`mobile-runtime`, which gates itself on the event and is not required (a skipped job satisfies a
required check).

The larger part is the strategy, and it started from the owner's question: is once every six months
enough? The answer turned out to be yes, but not for the reason it sounds like. **Security and
freshness are two problems on two clocks, and they had been mixed into one calendar** — which made
the calendar simultaneously too fast (five ignored PRs a fortnight) and too slow (a vulnerability
waiting for Monday).

What the audit found:

- **Dependabot alerts were on, but Dependabot _security updates_ were `disabled`.** An advisory
  would be reported and no pull request would ever be opened for it. That was the only real hole,
  and it is now enabled — a repository setting, not a file.
- **The runtime patches itself.** Every image floats on a minor tag (`dotnet/aspnet:10.0-alpine`,
  `sdk:10.0-alpine`, `pgvector:pg16`), and a merge to main deploys, which rebuilds. The bulk of CVEs
  by count arrive on the deploy cadence and were never waiting on a bot.
- **What actually rots is outside both lanes by design.** `expo ~55.0.9` against 57.x published,
  `react-native 0.83.10`, `targetSdkVersion 36` — excluded because they decide the OTA runtime
  fingerprint, and a bot bump there passes CI and then stops every installed app from receiving
  updates. Those carry the only dependency deadline with a consequence: Play's required target API,
  annually, on 31 August.

So: `deps-refresh.yml` became **one job, twice a year** (1 January, 1 July) proposing the lockfile
re-resolve, the pnpm catalog and `Directory.Packages.props` in one commit — down from two jobs, two
pull requests and a weekly cron. Dependabot's NuGet lane went to `open-pull-requests-limit: 0`,
which switches off version updates while leaving security updates untouched. `github-actions` stayed
with Dependabot, grouped into one pull request: `GITHUB_TOKEN` is refused permission to push under
`.github/workflows/**`, so our own workflow physically cannot raise an action pin.

Two things got deleted rather than written. The new job **verifies nothing itself** — it used to
re-run both site builds, the unit suites, typecheck and a Metro bundle, precisely because a bot pull
request got no CI. Now that CI runs on the branch, duplicating `expo install --check` and the Metro
bundle would buy ten minutes of nothing. And `scripts/nugetBump.mjs` imports `jump()` from
`catalogBump.mjs` rather than implementing semver distance a second time.

The NuGet bumper is `catalogBump.mjs`'s mirror image, for the same reasons: patch and minor yes,
major reported, line-by-line editing because `Directory.Packages.props` is more comment than data
and each comment records a resolution failure that cost something. It is called without
`--include-transitive` on purpose — the entries that exist only to pin a transitive
(`Microsoft.OpenApi`, held on the 2.x line) are numbers a human chose. One guard has no equivalent
on the pnpm side: `ModelContextProtocol` and `ModelContextProtocol.AspNetCore` must carry the same
version or the http transport desyncs from the protocol — it compiles, it starts, it fails on the
wire — so the pair moves together or not at all. Its first dry run against the real manifest
proposed 39 raises and held four majors, including the ImageSharp v4 that needs a paid licence.

Between the two runs a year, `scripts/report-drift.mjs` prints one line per ecosystem into the job
summary of every pull request — a gauge, never a gate, exit code always 0. It runs inside jobs that
already installed what it needs, so it costs seconds and no new job: `frontend` has the workspace,
`backend` has the restore, `mobile` has the app. The mobile line is the one that matters, because it
is the only thing that will ever mention Expo, and it says what Play requires and when the next
deadline falls. On the day it was written: JS 52 behind (38 major), NuGet 43 behind (4 major), and
`targetSdkVersion 36` compliant until 2027-08-31.

Deliberately not done: auto-merge on green (main deploys production, and two pull requests a year is
not a burden worth automating away), a PAT (the push trigger is the same outcome without a
credential), and a CI assertion that the security-updates setting is still on — it needs
`administration: read` and would 403 into a flaky gate, while `pnpm audit` in `advisories` and
`dotnet list package --vulnerable` in `backend` already fail the build on any advisory nobody has
written down. The auto-PR is a convenience on top of gates that already exist.

**Files**: `.github/workflows/deps-refresh.yml` (rewritten), `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
`.github/dependabot.yml`, `scripts/nugetBump.mjs`, `scripts/bump-nuget.mjs`,
`scripts/nugetBump.test.mjs`, `scripts/report-drift.mjs`.

<a id="2026-09-27-infra-a-patch-level-dependency-drift-had-blocked-every-deploy"></a>

## Infra — a patch-level dependency drift had blocked every deploy — mobile — 2026-09-27

`expo install --check` failed the `mobile` CI job on a single patch release:
`expo-updates@55.0.30` installed where the pinned `expo@55.0.31` expects `~55.0.31`. The pin read
`~55.0.20`, which permits the newer patch; the lockfile held the older one, so nothing moved on its
own.

The cost was not a red badge. `deploy.yml` declares `deploy: needs: ci`, so **a red `mobile` job
stops production deploys entirely** — and this one had been red since Expo published the patch,
across a fortnight in which nothing merged. The backup-verifier fix merged just before this one
therefore reached `main` and was never deployed; it only took effect at all because the nightly
backup runs the script from the runner's own checkout (`PROJECT_DIR`) rather than from a container.
A dependency check nobody was watching had quietly become a deploy freeze.

Fixed by pinning `~55.0.31` and letting the lockfile follow. `expo-updates` is a native module, so
this moves the runtime fingerprint — which costs nothing here, because
[#612](https://github.com/mrviduus/textstack/pull/612) added `expo-sharing` on 2026-09-14 and had
already closed the OTA path: the next mobile release has to be a build either way.

Worth noting for whoever reads the next freeze: the `mobile` job's failure is indistinguishable, at a
glance, from a code failure, and the thing it actually blocks is invisible from the PR page.

<a id="2026-09-27-ops-a-backup-verifier-that-failed-on-its-own-race-not-on-the-backup"></a>

## Ops — a backup verifier that failed on its own race, not on the backup — infra — 2026-09-27

The nightly backup went red for the first time in eleven days, on the step that exists to prove the
dump is restorable. The dump was restorable. The step that checks it was broken, and had been since
it was written — it simply won a coin toss every night until this one.

**What the log said, and why it was a lie.** `[verify] FAIL: postgres did not become ready after
120s`, printed **1.3 seconds** after the wait began. Both halves of that sentence pointed the wrong
way: the wait had not lasted 120 seconds, and postgres had in fact become ready. The message was a
constant, not a measurement, so the first thing anyone reading it would do — what the previous
backup incident taught them to do — is go looking for a full disk. The disk was at 69%.

**The race.** `backup-verify.sh` waited by polling `docker exec … pg_isready` with no `-h`, which
connects over the **Unix socket**. The postgres entrypoint starts *two* servers: a bootstrap server
that runs `initdb`, creates the database and executes `/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d`, and then, after
shutting that one down, the real one. The bootstrap server is started with `listen_addresses=''` —
socket only — precisely so that nothing outside the container can reach a database that is still
being built. A socket probe cannot tell the two apart. Captured from a container's own log:

```
[48] LOG:  listening on Unix socket "/var/run/postgresql/.s.PGSQL.5432"   <- bootstrap, socket only
[48] LOG:  database system is ready to accept connections                  24.397
[48] LOG:  received fast shutdown request                                  24.586
[1]  LOG:  listening on IPv4 address "0.0.0.0", port 5432                 <- real server
[1]  LOG:  database system is ready to accept connections                  24.838
```

So there is a ~190 ms window in which the old probe answered "ready" about a server that was about
to disappear. The loop `break`ed inside it — and then, because the loop was followed by a *second*
`pg_isready` as a safety net, that re-probe landed in the shutdown. The failing run matches to the
millisecond: bootstrap ready at `57.108`, shutdown request at `57.301`, `FAIL` printed at `57.42`.
The safety net was the entire bug. Without the re-probe the script would have gone on to restore
against a dying server and failed later, more confusingly.

**The fix is to ask a question only the real server can answer.** The probe now uses
`-h 127.0.0.1`: a TCP listener exists on the real server and never on the bootstrap one, so the
ambiguity is gone at the source rather than papered over with a longer sleep. Two *consecutive* hits
a second apart are required and the counter resets on a miss, which would also survive a transient
listener nobody has thought of yet. The break-then-re-probe pattern is deleted. And the failure
message now reports the elapsed wait it actually performed, because "after 120s" is what sent the
first ten minutes of this investigation to `df`.

**What was ruled out.** A corrupt or truncated dump (the file restores — see below). A full disk
(69%, and the diagnostics the previous incident added printed it). The 156 GB anonymous-volume leak
of [2026-07-10](../incidents/2026-07-10-backup-leaked-156gb.md), whose reaping code ran correctly
here. Docker reclaimable space was 28.8 GB, which is noise on 81 GB free.

**Verified end to end** on the self-hosted runner, against the same 1.9 GB dump the red run
produced: `postgres ready after 3s`, then `tables=63 indexes=242 users=7235 editions=1423
chapters=40589`, exit 0. That is the reassurance the red run failed to give: **the 2026-09-27
backup was always restorable.**

No incident postmortem for this one, deliberately. Nothing in production broke and nothing was lost
— a safety net reported a false negative for one night. It is written up here rather than in
`docs/incidents/` because the lesson is about how the check was written, not about how production
failed.

<a id="2026-09-14-offline-your-own-books-download-and-a-download-button-that-was-never-authorised"></a>

## Offline — your own books download, and a download button that was never authorised — mobile — 2026-09-14

Two reports from one reader, on a phone, about their own uploads: "there is no way to read offline",
and "I can't download — it says not authorised". Both were true, and neither was a regression: one
feature had never been built for uploads, and the other had never worked at all.

**Offline reading existed only for the catalogue.** `DownloadContext` and the SQLite cache
(`src/lib/offlineDb.ts`) were written for editions and keyed on `editionId`; the uploaded-book screen
had no download control, and `useUserBookReaderSource` fetched every chapter from the network with no
cache read behind it. For a product whose thesis is *user books first*, the half of the library that
matters most was the half with no offline story. Uploads now cache into a parallel pair of tables
(`user_chapters`, `cached_user_books`) — parallel rather than a `kind` column because an edition id is
public and shared while an upload id is private to one account, and the catalogue rows carry a route
slug an upload has no use for. The detail screen gains the same four-state control the catalogue book
has (download · downloading N% · retry the failed ones · downloaded/remove), the reader falls back to
the cache for the current chapter, the next chapter (infinite scroll picks up where the first load
leaves off, so without it an offline book stopped dead at the end of chapter one), the title, the
table of contents and the word counts, and the library tab lists downloaded uploads when it cannot
reach the server. The download loop is now written once and shared: only the fetch-and-store step
differs between the two libraries, and the retry budget, backoff and cancellation are not worth
having twice.

**A PDF upload downloads as its extracted text, and the button says so before the tap.** The
Original-layout viewer streams the file with Range requests and a Bearer token (ADR-012); there is no
offline form of that, and roughly 40% of this reader's library is PDF. Offline such a book opens in
the reflow reader over the cached chapters. That fallback creates a hazard worth naming: the session
produces a chapter-space position (`scroll:<slug>:<offset>`) while the book's stored position is a
page (`page:<N>`), so if the connection returns mid-chapter the ordinary progress write would
overwrite the reader's real page with a coordinate from a different space — the corruption
`readerWriteMode.ts` exists to prevent, arriving through the one door it does not watch. The server
write is suppressed for exactly that case; the local one still happens, so the offline session
resumes itself.

**"Download EPUB" could never have worked.** It called `Linking.openURL()` on
`<api>/me/books/{id}/export/epub` — the system browser, a different process, carrying none of the
app's credentials. The endpoint requires a Bearer token (`UserBooksEndpoints.ExportEpub` →
`Results.Unauthorized()`) and mobile has never had a cookie to fall back on, so every tap landed on a
401 and the reader read the word "unauthorized". The download now happens in-process with the token
attached (`expo-file-system`), refreshing an expiring token *before* spending it — the download API
reports failure as a bare thrown error, so a 401 after the fact is indistinguishable from being
offline — and the file is handed to the share sheet (`expo-sharing`, a new native dependency, hence a
new build rather than an OTA). Filenames are sanitised: these titles are whatever the file was called
when the reader found it, up to 200 characters of z-library provenance, and a name the filesystem
rejects fails the download after the bytes are already on the device.

**Two smaller things found on the way.** An offline chapter failure in an uploaded book reported
`'notfound'`, so a reader with no signal was told their book did not exist — the reader's own
offline empty state, which says "download the book for offline reading", had never been reachable for
uploads. And `getDb()` cached the database handle but not the in-flight open, so the detail screen and
the reader arriving on the same frame each opened their own connection and re-ran the schema script.

Signing out wipes cached uploads. The catalogue cache deliberately survives one — an edition is
public and the download belongs to the device — but an upload is one account's private file, and
leaving its text in SQLite would hand it to whoever signs in next.

Mobile `tsc` clean; **396 mobile unit tests green** (16 new, covering the filename sanitiser, the
chapter-key derivation the download and the reader route must agree on, and the cached-detail
rebuild — including what it refuses to claim it knows). Not device-verified at the time of writing:
the offline walk itself needs a phone in airplane mode.

<a id="2026-09-12-qa-three-regressions-in-code-shipped-the-day-before"></a>

## QA — three regressions in code shipped the day before — backend, mobile, shared — 2026-09-12

An adversarial pass over the nine PRs of 2026-09-11. Everything asserted about those screens was an
assertion about a projection function, because no mobile component test exists to run. Three of the
findings were regressions introduced by those PRs — all three mine, all three in the half of the work
that had no human looking at it.

**"Mark as finished" on the phone was a silent no-op on a slow clock.** The helper added to make web
and mobile write the *same* locator also sent `updatedAt: new Date().toISOString()` — a device clock.
The server reads that as a last-write-wins guard and, when it is not newer than the stored value,
answers **200 with the row untouched**. The shelf had already flipped optimistically, so the reader
saw "finished" and the server saw nothing. Web's `markAsRead` has never sent one. The guard is right
for a queued background sync and wrong for a button; the timestamp is gone, and a test asserts the
payload does not carry one while the ordinary progress write still does.

**The Apple sign-in never warned about a dropped guest merge.** #609 wired `warnIfNothingCarried`
into three of the four merge entry points. An iOS reader whose guest row was not merged was landed in
a new account with no indication that their highlights, vocabulary and progress had stayed behind.
A literal-scanning test now covers all four, in the shape `capabilityLiterals` and `routeLiterals`
already use — because mobile has no screen test that could.

**The Tutor kept ordering a tool that does not exist.** #605 removed `get_example_sentence` from the
allowed list and from the system prompt, and missed the copy in `BuildGoal` — sent on **every**
re-plan turn. The guard added with it could not see it: it reflects over a string array, while the
instruction is prose.

So the guard learned to read. It now scans what the agent *says* for anything shaped like a tool name
and requires it to be allowed. Two false starts are worth recording: matching against *registered*
tools left the mutation green (a deleted tool is registered nowhere, which is exactly the case that
hurts), and a naive scan failed on a correct file because these prompts wrap at 110 columns and a
name lands across a concatenation. It now glues adjacent literals, and both mutations — the plain one
and the split one — turn it red.

<a id="2026-09-11-guests-the-reader-is-told-when-their-work-did-not-come-across"></a>

## Guests — the reader is told when their work did not come across — web, mobile, shared — 2026-09-11

A guest reads, highlights, saves words. They sign in. If the merge is abandoned — an expired guest
token, or a unique-index collision on `reading_sessions` — the sign-in still succeeds, by design: a
throw there would be a permanent sign-in outage, since the client re-presents the same guest token on
every retry. The server says so in the response, as `guestMergeSkipped`.

**No client had ever read that field.** So the reader landed in their new account with everything from
before missing, and the web app showed them a toast saying *"Your reading progress and saved words
were kept."* The one sentence that must not be said in that moment, because it is the sentence that
stops them looking.

Both clients read it now. Web shows the warning **instead of** the reassurance — the precedence is a
pure function, `authToastFor`, so the mutual exclusion is a test rather than an ordering of two `if`s
that someone will later reorder. Mobile shows it after any of its three sign-in paths (email,
register, Google), on a long timer, as a toast rather than a dialog: they *are* signed in, and there
is nothing for them to decide.

One sentence, one key (`guest.progressNotCarried`), shared by both apps — web's catalogue merges the
shared one, so a second local copy would have been two places to fix a wording nobody would keep in
step.

Both skip reasons say the same thing to the reader; they differ only for whoever reads the logs. And
the message says what is true — the work stayed on the previous session — rather than claiming it was
deleted or promising it can be recovered.

<a id="2026-09-11-sentry-a-developer-machine-is-not-an-incident"></a>

## Sentry — a developer machine is not an incident — backend, mobile — 2026-09-11

Three issues sat at the top of the feed: `HTTP 401 invalid_api_key` on `/explain` and the tutor,
**141 events over a month**, last seen six hours earlier. They were read as a production outage and
chased as one — twice, with two wrong diagnoses on the way.

They were not production. `environment: Development`, `url: http://localhost/explain`, and the key in
the message ended `…vXgA` — the same last six characters as the stale key in this machine's local
`.env`. Production was fine the whole time: a live call to `/api/translate` on a guaranteed
cache-miss and a live `/api/explain` both returned real answers from OpenAI in seconds.

So the local runs of an API — every rebuild, every integration-test pass — were writing into the
account that exists to tell us when production breaks. The events were indistinguishable from real
ones at a glance, which is the whole problem: an error tracker is only worth having if everything in
it is real.

`SentryBootstrap.Resolve` now returns null for `Development`, which is the existing no-op contract —
the hosts never engage the SDK at all. The mobile app applies the same rule. The DSN deliberately
stays in the local `.env`: the point is that having it configured is harmless, not that it must be
deleted and re-pasted. `Sentry:EnableInDevelopment` (and `EXPO_PUBLIC_SENTRY_IN_DEV` on mobile) opts
back in, for the one case that wants dev events — working on this integration itself.

The mobile rule moved into its own importless module to be testable at all: `sentry.ts` pulls in
`@sentry/react-native` and through it React Native's Flow-typed entry point, which the test runner
cannot parse. Both sides pin that a half-set flag (`1`, `yes`, `TRUE`) does **not** count as opting
in.

<a id="2026-09-11-genres-a-field-two-clients-used-and-the-server-never-sent"></a>

## Genres — a field both clients used and the server never sent — backend, mobile, shared — 2026-09-11

Sentry, on a reader's OnePlus: `TypeError: Cannot read property 'map' of undefined`, in `GenreScreen`.

`GET /genres/{slug}` projected each book as id, slug, title, language, cover — **no authors at
all**. The mobile genre screen called `ed.authors.map(a => a.name)`, so opening *any* genre that has
books threw. It is caught rather than fatal, so what the reader saw was a screen that did not render
instead of a crash — which is why it survived a year.

The web page had the same field and the same absence, and wrote `ed.authors || []`. It did not throw;
its "Popular authors in <genre>" section has simply been empty since it was written.

**Why nothing caught it.** The shared TypeScript type declared these editions as the full `Edition`
shape, and the endpoint sends a narrow projection. A type that describes something the server does
not send is worse than no type: it moves the error from the compiler to the reader's phone. There is
now a `ListedEdition` that says what genre and author pages actually receive — and the moment it
landed, `tsc` pointed at the exact line Sentry had reported.

The projection now sends authors, ordered by `EditionAuthor.Order`, using the same `BookAuthorDto`
the book list uses so a book's authors read identically wherever it appears. Two broken things come
back at once: the author under each card on the phone, and the web's dead section.

`GenreEndpointTests` asserts the wire — every edition carries an `authors` **array**, and at least one
book in the catalogue actually names someone, so the test cannot pass on a projection that sends
empty arrays. It fails against the previous build and passes against this one. A unit test could not
have caught this: the defect was in an EF projection, and the only question that matters is what
reaches the client.

<a id="2026-09-11-tutor-the-planned-exercise-becomes-the-card"></a>

## Tutor — the exercise it plans is now the card you get — backend, web, mobile — 2026-09-11

The Tutor calibrates an exercise type for every word from that card's SRS stage: `recognition` for a
new word, `recall` at stage 2, `context` for a well-known one with a sentence. It is computed on the
server, deliberately *not* taken from the model's suggestion so the plan cannot schedule a jarring
jump, carried all the way to the client with everything needed to render it — and then thrown away.
`buildPlanCard` hardcoded `options: null` and `blankSentence: null`, so all three types produced the
same flashcard and the calibration reached the learner as a coloured badge.

Now the shape follows the plan:

- **`recognition`** — four options, no sentence prompt. Which word means this.
- **`recall`** — no options at all. A flashcard the learner grades against themselves, which is the
  point of that stage; four options would be a recognition exercise wearing a recall label.
- **`context`** — four options *and* the saved sentence with the word removed. The cloze, in the
  sentence the word was actually met in.

**One implementation of what the choices are.** The options come from `McOptions`, extracted here
from `ReviewCardBuilder` — which carried **two copies** of the same forty lines, one for
`multiple_choice` and one for `context`. The builder is now 61 lines instead of 145, the rule has one
home and three callers, and it finally has tests: the answer is always among the four, never
duplicated as its own distractor, and the cascade prefers the LLM's distractors, then the learner's
own vocabulary, then a hardcoded list so a card is never short of options (three choices would
themselves tell the learner something).

For the Tutor the distractor pool is **the other words in the same session** — words the learner has
actually met, which make a real choice rather than obvious filler.

The clients choose the component from the payload (`options`) rather than from the type string, so a
card can never claim options it does not carry. `reviewMode` stays vestigial and is still not read by
anything: the shared DTO's comment says so and now stays true.

<a id="2026-09-11-tutor-an-instruction-that-could-not-be-obeyed"></a>

## Tutor — an instruction the model could not obey, on every run — backend — 2026-09-11

The Tutor's system prompt told the model: *"On a word the learner missed (or a hard context-stage
card), call `get_example_sentence` to ground it in a real sentence from their reading."*

That tool was deleted with the retrieval spine. `ToolRegistry.SchemasFor` skips an unknown name
**silently** — deliberately, so a half-configured agent still runs — so the model was never offered
it, nothing failed, and nothing logged. Every planning run since has paid for an instruction that
could only be ignored. An instruction that cannot be obeyed is worse than a missing feature.

Removed from the allowed-tools list and from the prompt, along with the eval's stub for it and two
doc comments still describing it as live.

**Grounding is not lost, and getting it back needs no tool.** `VocabularyWord.Sentence` already holds
the sentence each word was saved from, and `get_due_vocabulary` already reports `hasSentence` per
card — the sentence itself belongs in that same projection, which costs one round-trip *fewer* than a
dedicated tool did.

**The guard matters more than the fix.** `AgentAllowedToolsExistTests` reflects over every agent's
`AllowedTools` and asserts each name is implemented by a real `ITool`, reading names off the concrete
types rather than booting the API's composition root. It is verified by mutation — putting the dead
name back turns it red — and it includes a check that the reflection found any agents at all, because
a reflection-driven theory that finds nothing passes vacuously, which is this style of test's own
failure mode.
<a id="2026-09-11-guests-a-middleware-that-never-ran"></a>

## Guests — a middleware that never ran once — backend — 2026-09-11

`GuestActivityMiddleware` exists to keep `users.LastActiveAt` true for guests, so the cleanup worker
does not delete someone who is still reading. It decided whether a request belonged to a guest by
reading `context.User.FindFirst("is_guest")`.

This API registers **no ASP.NET authentication middleware at all** — by design; every endpoint
resolves identity by hand through `GetUserId`. So `HttpContext.User` is permanently empty, the check
was always false, and the method returned on its first line for every request it ever served.
`LastActiveAt` was written exactly once per guest, at creation.

The claim was never missing. `AuthService` has minted `is_guest` into every guest's access token
since guest sessions shipped — `ValidateAccessToken` simply threw it away, returning only the id. It
now has a sibling, `ValidateAccessTokenIdentity`, that returns both, and the claim name is a constant
read by the one place that mints it and the one that reads it.

**What the damage actually was**, stated precisely rather than dramatically: `GuestCleanupWorker`'s
preservation filter also spares any guest holding a `ReadingProgress` row, so a guest who genuinely
read a book survived regardless. The exposure was a guest who opened books and never produced a
progress row — and a pipeline carrying a slice that did nothing, under a paragraph in `Program.cs`
reasoning carefully about where to place it.

Two things changed beyond the identity fix. The old debounce read the user row on **every** guest
request to decide whether to skip the update — the read it was trying to avoid; it is now an
`IMemoryCache` entry, so the common path touches no database at all. And the write is a targeted
`ExecuteUpdateAsync` rather than loading an entity, so nothing else on the row can be written by
accident.

**Verified as a side effect, not as a 200** — the lesson this same middleware taught the first time.
Against a live stack: a guest request moves `last_active_at`; five more requests in the debounce
window move it not at all; an account's row is never touched. The decision itself is now a pure
function with tests, which the previous version had no seam for.

<a id="2026-09-10-insights-a-conclusion-can-be-removed"></a>

## Insights — a conclusion filed against the wrong chapter can finally be removed — backend, web, mobile — 2026-09-10

Saving again replaces the insight for the same chapter, so a *poor* conclusion about the right
chapter fixes itself on the next pass. A conclusion filed against the **wrong** chapter does not:
nothing ever revisits that slot, the assistant only overwrites the chapter it meant, and no client
could remove it. It was permanent.

`DELETE /me/insights/{id}`, reader-only. 404 rather than 403 for someone else's id, so the endpoint
cannot be used to learn that an id exists. Hard delete — the entity's own stance is a конспект, not a
log, and a `deleted_at` would have to enter both partial unique index filters, which is where the
`NULLS NOT DISTINCT` rule protecting the book-level row lives.

**Deliberately not an MCP tool.** `save_insight`'s worst case is one bad paragraph the reader removes
in a tap. A delete tool's worst case is a year of конспект gone, driven by a stateless bridge that
cannot confirm intent, against a table with no soft-delete and no trash. That asymmetry is
categorical, not a matter of degree.

On both clients the affordance sits beside the chapter label rather than in a corner of its own —
removing a note is a rare correction, not something the panel should advertise. A delete the server
refuses leaves the row on screen, because it is still there.

This came out of a three-way consilium on insight categories, which agreed on the delete and
disagreed on everything else; what it settled and what it left for the owner is written up in
[`assistant-handoff.md`](../05-features/assistant-handoff.md).

**Follow-up, same day — the categories were dropped before they were built.** The plan called for
four fixed categories (Conclusions · Watch for · Discussed · Questions) with tabs. Three independent
readings of the code disagreed about them and converged on one question for the owner: *when you come
back in a month, do you open one book or all of them?* The answer was one book — and per-book
retrieval is served by a chapter label and reading order, both of which already shipped. No column,
no migration, no tabs. What the panel was genuinely missing was a **date**: a конспект is read months
later, and without one it cannot answer "is this what I thought then, or what I think now". That is
one pure function (`insightDateLabel`), absolute rather than relative, ISO so it is unambiguous in
every locale. The enum is revisitable if retrieval ever turns cross-book — the one shape that really
needs a typed field.

<a id="2026-09-10-progress-the-same-action-writes-the-same-thing"></a>

## Progress — the same action writes the same thing, and the first write stops being the exception — backend, web, mobile, shared — 2026-09-10

Four of the ten progress-path defects recorded while tracing the assistant handoff, closed. Two of
the remaining six turn out not to be worth fixing, and saying why is the more useful outcome than
fixing them would have been.

**"Mark as finished" wrote two different things.** Web sent the `{"type":"end"}` sentinel; mobile,
from the same menu on the same kind of book, sent `scroll:<lastSlug>:0` — so a book marked finished
on the phone reopened at the top of its last chapter and the same book marked finished in the browser
did not. The sentinels now have one definition (`packages/shared/src/reader/progressLocators.ts`),
both clients import it, and mobile's action goes through a `markProgressFinished` that does not
pretend to know a scroll offset it was never told.

**The first write for a book was the one that skipped the rules.** `PUT /me/progress/{editionId}`
had two code paths writing one row: an update that checked `ProgressUnit.IsTrusted` before storing a
percentage, and an insert that assigned `request.Percent` by hand. So the very first write for an
edition kept an untrusted number every later write would have refused — and a book finished in a
single write never got a `CompletedAt`, because the insert did not record completion either. The
insert now builds an empty row and hands it to the same `ApplyProgressUpdate`.
`ProgressUnitEndpointTests` covers it from outside, because which branch runs is a database fact no
unit test can choose.

**`ReadingProgressDto` existed twice in TypeScript** with nothing linking the copies. Web now imports
the shared one and re-exports it, so every `from '../api/auth'` import keeps working against a single
definition.

**Two are deliberately left alone.**

- **`LocatorKind` on the catalog path.** The guard exists because an upload can be read two ways —
  Original-layout PDF (`page:<n>`) and reflow (`scroll:…`). A catalog edition has no PDF and no second
  space. Adding it would break exactly what it was meant to protect: `MayReplace` refuses any write
  whose locator belongs to no space, and the mark-as-read sentinel above is precisely that.
- **`markAsUnread` never lowering `MaxChapterNumber`.** Its only reader was the RAG spoiler gate,
  deleted with `ask_book`. The column is now written by two paths and read by none — a drop
  candidate, not a bug, and dropping a column on a production table to correct a value nobody reads
  is the more expensive mistake.

**And a tap that did nothing now says so.** "Talk this book over" swallowed `openURL`'s rejection on
purpose — there is nothing useful to say about "you have no browser". But the outcome a reader sees
is a button that does nothing, which is indistinguishable from a broken one. It shows a toast.

**Five dead web modules and one stylesheet deleted** — `fuzzyMatch`, `wordAtPoint`,
`useOfflineDownload`, `useSwipe`, `useVocabLevel`, `native-language-picker.css`. No importers, and
none on `main` either. Three of them were listed as live in `CLAUDE.md`'s own hook inventory.
<a id="2026-09-10-mcp-the-assistant-can-see-where-you-are"></a>

## MCP — the assistant can see where you are, and record where you got to — backend — 2026-09-10

Thirteen tools could read any book in the library and write conclusions back into it. Not one of them
could answer *where the reader is*. The position existed on the server the whole time; the only way it
ever surfaced was as `ask_book`'s spoiler refusal, and that tool is gone. So a conversation about a
book began from nothing every time: which book, which chapter, how far — all of it re-typed by the
person who had just been reading it.

Three tools, no migration.

**`get_my_reading` takes no arguments.** That is the point of it: every other tool needs an id, and
nothing produced one. It answers over `GET /me/library/shelves`, which has always returned both book
kinds in one response with titles, authors and progress already joined, and which no MCP tool called.
Each row hands back the id the other tools actually take — `bookId` for an upload, `editionId` *and*
`slug` for a catalog book — because handing back the wrong one reads as "book not found" three calls
later, far from the cause. The shelf is capped and filtered to in-progress, so `GET /me/books`
supplies the rest, including books never opened.

**`get_book_progress`** answers the same question for one book: the chapter you stopped in, how far
in, whether you finished it. A book never opened 404s upstream, which is an answer, not a failure —
reporting it as an error would have the model tell a reader their library is broken.

**`set_book_progress` closes the loop the other way.** You listened to chapter two in Spotify; the app
should know. It takes a chapter *slug*, and what it does with it is the part worth stating: finishing
a chapter resumes you at the **start of the next one** — not back in what you just finished — using
the app's own two sentinels (`{"type":"start"}`, and `{"type":"end"}` on the last chapter, which is
exactly what "mark as read" writes). Progress becomes chapters-finished over chapters-total, declared
as a book fraction, because the catalog path *assigns* that column: a write carrying no number blanks
the one that was there.

**Four defects had to be fixed for any of this to be true.**

- **A chapter slug was stored unvalidated.** `UserBookService.UpsertProgressAsync` assigned
  `request.ChapterSlug` raw. An assistant that invented a slug had it stored verbatim, and every later
  read — resume, shelf card, reader — resolved it to nothing. The bookmark path in the same service
  has always checked. Progress never did.
- **A refusal reported success.** `LocatorSpace.MayReplace` returning false dropped the whole write
  and answered `(true, null)`. That was defensible while the only callers were readers' apps, which
  cannot act on an error and would retry into it. It stopped being defensible the moment an assistant
  became a caller: a 200 that stored nothing has it tell a person their progress was recorded.
- **`get_book`'s chapter projection dropped the chapter id** — while `save_highlight`'s own
  description told the model to take `chapterId` "from `get_book`". Following that instruction was
  impossible. The id is back, which is also what lets the catalog write resolve a slug to the GUID the
  route requires.
- **The shelf selected `chapterSlug` and threw it away**, because `LibraryShelfItemDto` had nowhere to
  put it. One optional field at the end of the record; the service already had the value in all three
  projections.

Proven against the running bridge rather than only the stubs: mint a key, `POST /mcp`, `get_my_reading`
returns the shelf **with titles**, `set_book_progress` moves the position to the next chapter, the next
`get_my_reading` shows it moved, and an invented slug comes back as an error instead of a silent save.

**Tool count is asserted in four places** (`McpManifestDriftTests`, `McpStdioSmokeTests`,
`McpOverTheWireTests`, `McpManifestEndpointTests`) and every description is mirrored character for
character into `Contracts/Mcp/McpManifest.cs`, which the API serves and the drift test compares.

<a id="2026-09-08-mcp-the-book-opens-to-your-assistant-and-the-conclusions-come-back"></a>

## MCP — the book opens to your assistant, and the conclusions come back — backend, web — 2026-09-08

A colleague filed a small bug: MCP could not reach a book the user had uploaded. It was real, and the
fix it proposed was not buildable. Chasing why produced a bigger answer about what TextStack is.

**The bug, and why the spec could not be followed.** Step one of the spec — add a library-search
endpoint — was already done: `GET /me/library/search` has existed, scoped by `user_id` in SQL, and
the web already uses it. Step two asked the tool to return an `editionId` for each hit. **An upload
does not have one and cannot be given one.** `UserBook` and `Edition` are separate aggregates with
separate chapter and chunk tables, and the non-polymorphism is documented on the entity itself. Built
as written, every id would 404 in `ask_book` and return a silent empty list from
`list_my_highlights` — a wrong id that reads like an empty library. The identifier is `bookId`, and
the tool descriptions now say so in as many words.

**Why "I connected Claude Code and got nothing" was not a prompt problem.** Reconnaissance on
production: 75 uploaded books, **3 indexed**; 1,423 catalog editions, **4** with chunks. An
unindexed book returns `insufficient` *without calling the LLM*. `ask_book` was answering correctly
about a corpus that was 96% empty. Cost was never the constraint — every conversational feature
together has spent five cents; $4.14 of the $4.25 total is PDF vision parsing.

**Six tools for the uploaded half, needing no index at all.** `search_my_library` (Postgres FTS over
`user_chapters.search_vector`, built at upload), `get_my_book`, `get_my_chapter`,
`save_my_highlight`, `list_my_book_highlights`. Measured on production: **73 of 75 books have
chapters and 72 have real text**, so this works on essentially the whole library today, with no
embeddings and no vision parse. `search_books`' description now says PUBLIC catalog and names its
counterpart, because two search tools whose names differ by `_my_` is not enough for a model under
pressure.

**And then the part that is not a bug fix.** We are not building the chat. A reader's assistant
already holds their profile, their memory and a year of conversation; that cannot be copied here and
competing with it from an empty corpus is not a plan. So: **the reasoning happens there, the result
comes home.** New entity `BookInsight` (table `book_insight`) plus `save_insight` / `get_my_insights`
and `GET|POST /me/insights`. A conclusion hangs on the book's spine — a chapter slug, or nothing for
the whole book — the way every other piece of user markup already does.

A catalog, not a transcript: a transcript is ordered by time and useless to return to, because the
conclusion you want is in the fortieth message. A study конспект is therefore an *assembly* of these
in reading order rather than a frozen document, which is why one insight is kept per
(user, book, chapter) and a save replaces. That upsert has a Postgres trap in it: by default NULLs in
a unique index are distinct from one another, and `chapter_slug IS NULL` is the book-level
overview — the single row most likely to be rewritten, and so the only one a default index would
fail to protect. The index is `NULLS NOT DISTINCT`, and an integration test says so.

The key is the **slug**, not the chapter id: re-ingestion deletes and recreates every chapter, so a
`chapterId` comes unstuck. The same lesson as [ADR-015](../01-architecture/adr/ADR-015-reader-position-is-logical.md),
learned the same week. Chapter number and title are resolved from the slug **at read time**, so a
re-ingest that renumbers chapters cannot leave a stale ordinal behind, and a slug that no longer
resolves keeps its text and sorts last rather than vanishing.

**On the book page**, a "What you've worked out" section renders those conclusions above the chapter
list — coming back to a book, what you already concluded beats the table of contents — and a "Talk
this book over" pair of links opens a new Claude or ChatGPT conversation with a brief already
written. That is a link, not an integration, and it is capped at 1,200 characters because a URL is
about 2,000 and percent-encoding eats the rest; the book does not travel in the link, the connector
fetches it. Without a connector it is still a real conversation about a real book — the reader just
carries the outcome back by hand.

Verified live, not only in tests: the whole loop through the actual stdio MCP server against a
running API — 14 tools listed, insight written, chapter title resolved on read-back, book-level entry
first, both-identifiers refused, another user seeing nothing. 1,533 unit + 20 MCP-wire + 771 web
tests green; 13 new integration tests against the live API.

**Second pass over the gaps that shipped with it.** Four of the five things flagged as open turned
out to be worth acting on, and checking them corrected two of my own claims.

*The write ceiling.* `save_my_highlight` had a per-call limit and nothing else, so a client told to
mark what matters could mark a book to illegibility. Now `HighlightsEndpoints` caps a book at **200
assistant-placed highlights**, counted by `anchor_json->>'source' = 'mcp'` — the field the bridge
already writes — so a person highlighting heavily is never counted, including on a book an assistant
has also marked. Verified by seeding 200 rows: the 201st is refused, a human anchor on the same book
still saves. The first attempt used `.Contains()`, which EF compiles to `LIKE`; `anchor_json` is
jsonb and Postgres refuses `LIKE` against it outright, so that would have thrown on the first write
past the gate. Reading the field is also independent of whitespace and key order, which a substring
match is not, and a drift test now asserts both sides agree — the two live in different projects with
an opaque column between them, and a cap that silently stops matching is the worst way for a limit to
fail.

`POST /me/highlights` also gains a rate limit, and it is the **only policy in the codebase partitioned
by user id rather than IP**. The endpoint is reached both by a person in the reader and by the MCP
bridge, which talks to the API over the internal docker network — so every assistant write in the
deployment arrives from one container address, and an IP key would let one looping client throttle
every other MCP user. Verified: user A throttled after 116 writes in a window, user B from the same
IP in the same second still served.

*"Not your book" versus "nothing here."* `GET /me/insights` filtered by `user_id` and so leaked
nothing, but answered a stranger's book with `[]` — and an empty list is a truthful answer to "what
have I worked out about this book", which lets a wrong id read as a fact. It checks the book first
now, like `GET /me/highlights/userbook/{id}` does, and the bridge distinguishes the two.

*A claim of mine that was wrong.* I reported that `save_my_highlight` could not reach a chapterless
PDF upload. Production says otherwise: the two books with no chapters are **failed ingests**, not
chapterless PDFs, and every readable book has chapters. But checking it found the real version of the
problem, which is worse and affects **half the library** — 36 of 75 uploads are PDFs, the reader shows
a PDF as the original document (ADR-012), and `PdfHighlightLayer` only paints `kind:"pdf"` anchors.
A text-anchored highlight from MCP is therefore saved, listed, and shown on the Highlights page, but
never drawn over the page. `get_my_book` now reports `rendersAsOriginalPdf` and the tool description
says what happens, so the model can tell you rather than leaving you hunting for a mark that was never
going to appear.

*And one that was understated.* I called `chapter_number` "0-based for editions, 1-based for user
chapters". It is neither: production has every edition starting at 0 but **four uploaded books
starting at 2**, and locally five editions start at 1. It is an ordering key that was never a display
ordinal, `BookDetailPage` renders `chapterNumber + 1` and `UserBookDetailPage` renders it raw, and
both are wrong for the exceptions. Written down in `CLAUDE.md` rather than papered over with a
migration nobody asked for.

**And then the feature was pointed at a real uploaded book, which is when it broke.** Everything up
to this point was exercised against canned JSON and a catalog book, because the local database had no
uploads. Clipping an article to make one turned `search_my_library` up empty on a book that plainly
contained the search term.

`GET /me/library/search` was answering **500 on every call, and always had been.** Its raw SQL aliases
its columns in PascalCase, the context is built with `UseSnakeCaseNamingConvention`, and that
convention applies to the `SqlQueryRaw` row type too — so EF asked for `chapter_slug`, was handed
`"ChapterSlug"`, and threw before reading a row. Nobody had noticed because the only caller is the web
Library page's search box, which renders an empty result on failure: a library search that finds
nothing looks exactly like a library search that failed. So the colleague's bug report was righter
than it knew — the endpoint their spec called step 1, and called done, has never returned a result.
Aliases are snake_case now, both SQL branches, with an integration test that asserts a 200, because
only real SQL against a real database can catch it.

The bridge was making it worse in its own way: `SearchMyLibraryAsync` mapped any non-2xx to an empty
list, so a 500 reached the model as "you have no books about this" — the same confident-and-wrong
answer this work fixed twice elsewhere and left standing here. Failure and emptiness are separate
answers now.

Two smaller things the same run found. `list_my_book_highlights` reported `chapterId: null` for every
highlight on an upload, because an upload's chapter lives in `userChapterId` — so a model could not
tell what it had marked where, which is most of the point of listing them before marking more. And
`get_my_book` is where `rendersAsOriginalPdf` had to go, since that is the call a model makes before
writing anything.

*The one left alone.* The integration suite trips its own rate limits locally, because CI raises
`CLIP_PERMIT_LIMIT` and friends and a developer's `.env` does not. Putting the raised values in
`.env.example` would weaken production for anyone who copies it, so `CLAUDE.md` now carries the
command instead — along with the sharper trap: the windows are minutes long, so re-running a suite to
confirm a failure is what causes the next one.

**Deliberately not in this work:** semantic retrieval and the indexing tools (they matter for 3 books
until indexing is decided); bulk indexing, whose cost is not embeddings — a quarter of a cent per
book — but the PDF vision parse at $0.00586 a page; the fate of our own Book Chat, which is a focus
decision and should not be smuggled inside a bug fix.

<a id="2026-09-07-docs-adr-015-and-the-scenario-that-was-never-in-qa-001"></a>

## Docs — ADR-015, and the scenario that was never in QA-001 — docs — 2026-09-07

The record for the position work, and the reason it took sixteen commits to get here.

**[ADR-015](../01-architecture/adr/ADR-015-reader-position-is-logical.md)** states the model and,
more usefully, states what was already decided and not done: ADR-007 chose a logical position in
January 2026 and listed `scrollY` among the things explicitly not used; `scroll:<slug>:<pixelOffset>`
shipped four months later. ADR-007 now says so, pointing forward. ADR-013 records that its rules are
unchanged and still govern the locator — rule 3 is precisely *why* the position became a column
rather than a third space.

**QA-001 never crossed a chapter boundary.** Its four steps navigate by TOC and by the library card,
and both of those stay inside the chapter the URL names. The mobile reader appends the next chapter
into the same document as you scroll, which is where every reported "it forgot where I was" has
lived — so the suite passed for eight months while readers lost their place. Five steps added,
marked UNTESTED because they are: read across a boundary and change the font size, then kill the
app, then the same offline, then cross-device and against an old build, then a PDF in Original
layout to confirm it is untouched.

**One thing was declined rather than built.** Making the route follow the reader would remove the
URL/document disagreement at source, and while the position was a pixel that disagreement caused
data loss. It no longer can: an anchor names its own chapter, so a position cannot be applied to the
wrong one — `resolveTextPosition` returns null and the caller changes route instead. What remained
was cosmetics on a client with no address bar, against surgery on the reset key of
`useReaderPersistence` and the identity of the WebView document, which is where all four of #496,
#500, #501 and the font-size defect lived. `readerWriteTarget.test.ts` pins the property the surgery
would have made structural, so if the reasoning stops holding, a test says so.

<a id="2026-09-07-library-the-shelf-that-could-not-continue"></a>

## Library — the shelf that could not continue — backend, shared — 2026-09-07

Slice 6, and the smallest change with the longest history.

`LibraryShelvesService` has always SELECTED the progress row's locator —
`CurrentLocator = latestProgress.Locator` — and then thrown it away, because `LibraryShelfItemDto`
had nowhere to put it. So the shelf named "Continue Reading" could show how far into a book a reader
was and structurally could not offer to continue from there; the client had to make a second request
per book to find out. `GET /me/books` had the same hole: a percentage and a chapter slug, no
position.

Both DTOs now carry the locator and the logical position. `rankContinueReading` exists because of
exactly this gap — its docstring says so — and can now be given the whole answer rather than
reassembling it.

While there: `pickUserBook` was the last place still reading a chapter slug without consulting the
locator beside it. It has not cost anything yet, because that slug is client-written rather than
server-derived — but one branch of one function obeying a rule the other branch does not is how
#496 became #500 became #501, and the comment above it now says which rule and why.

<a id="2026-09-07-reader-web-joins-the-position-model"></a>

## Reader — web joins the position model, and stops splitting on colons — web — 2026-09-07

Slice 4. The same model on the other client, plus two defects found by looking.

**Web had the same disease without the amplifier.** It renders one chapter per route and applies
typography as inline styles on the `<article>` — no remount, no reload, nothing to lose. So the text
simply re-wrapped under a fixed `scrollTop`, the reader moved, and the debounced save then wrote
where they had been moved to. Nothing in `useReaderScrollSync` has ever depended on `settings`; that
absence *was* the defect. It now captures the reading line before the reflow and re-applies it after
— signalled by a `ResizeObserver` on the article, because a style change fires no `resize` on window
— and re-primes the skip window so the transient is never saved.

**`locator.split(':')`.** Web parsed the locator inline while `parseScrollLocator` sat in
`@textstack/shared` being used only by mobile. The shared one reads from the RIGHT precisely because
a chapter slug is derived from a user-supplied title: `scroll:act-i:scene-ii:900` gave web a slug of
`act-i` and `parseInt('scene-ii')` → NaN → offset 0. Two implementations of one format, one of them
wrong, which is the thing `@textstack/shared` exists to prevent. There is a test for the colon now.

**The keepalive write sent no `percentUnit`.** `flushSave` builds its body by hand rather than going
through `upsertProgress`, which adds the declaration for every other writer — so the LAST write
before a tab closes, the one most likely to be the newest the server ever sees, silently failed to
store its percentage (`ProgressUnit` drops an undeclared one). The comment on the user-book twin says
this is "exactly how it came to be missing one of them"; the catalog twin had it too.

**Also smaller.** The three copies of the save — save-on-open, the debounced scroll save, the
keepalive flush — each built the locator itself and each branched on mode. They are one `writeProgress`
now, which is why threading the text position through cost one call site rather than three.

The e2e that has never existed exists — and cost two wrong assumptions before it did. It first
assumed a chapter long enough to scroll; CI seeds a single short paragraph. It then assumed the
saved anchor comes back byte-identical after a reflow; it does not, and should not — the reading
line falls on a different character of the same passage. It asserts what a reader actually depends
on: a position is still saved, it still names this chapter, and the passage it quotes is still in
the text. That fails if the save writes null, another chapter, or an anchor pointing at nothing.

**And the short chapter was not just a bad fixture — it was a defect.** The reading line is a
quarter down the viewport, so a chapter that ends above it — a poem, a preface, a clip, the stub
last chapter of a book — yielded no caret, no anchor, and silently no logical position at all,
falling back to the pixel offset forever. Measured in a browser: a one-paragraph chapter ends at
108px with the line at 200px. The probe is now clamped into the chapter's own visible band, on both
clients.

<a id="2026-09-07-reader-the-position-is-a-place-in-the-text"></a>

## Reader — the position is a place in the text — mobile, backend, shared — 2026-09-07

Slices 2 and 3 of the reading-position work. Slice 1 stopped a font change destroying the reader's
place; this is the model that stops a pixel being the answer at all.

**What a position is now.** `TextPosition` in `@textstack/shared`: a chapter slug, a text anchor
(30 characters of context either side of a 64-character quote), a character-offset hint and a
chapter fraction. Which is to say: a highlight without a colour. The resolver is the one highlights
have used since it was written — `findAnchorOffset`, whose own docstring names this case: *"A
highlight, a bookmark **or a reading position** cannot be stored as a pixel offset or a character
index."*

**Why not `paragraph_index`, which is what ADR-007 proposed.** The reason is specific rather than
general, and worth stating: the index of a `<p>` within a chapter survives a font change perfectly
well — it is a DOM fact, not a text fact. What it does not survive is re-ingestion, which deletes
and recreates every chapter from freshly parsed HTML, and the two clients sanitising differently. A
text anchor survives both.

**Why a column and not a third locator space.** `text:<slug>:<offset>` was the obvious shape and it
is a trap. ADR-013 rule 3 — *same space, accept, declared or not* — is the entire compatibility
story for already-installed builds. Introduce a third space and the first new client to write it
turns every old client's `scroll:` write into an undeclared cross-space write, refused by rule 5: a
phone that updated would silently stop a desktop that had not from saving anything. On the catalog
path, which `MayReplace` does not guard at all, it is worse — the old client's write lands, and the
old client cannot parse what it overwrote. So the position went into `reading_progresses.position_json`
and `user_books.progress_position_json`, beside a locator that is still written in the old form.
`LocatorSpaceTests` now asserts that `Derive("text:…")` is *still null*, so the trap cannot be walked
into later by someone who does not know it is one.

**One invariant, and it is the whole server-side job:** a row never holds two positions that
disagree. A write carrying no position CLEARS the stored one — not "keeps what was there", because
