container that runs continuously in production. An unsupported runtime, receiving no security
patches, serving the site, with nothing anywhere positioned to say so.

There was no single source of truth to be wrong about. Seven declarations carried four answers:

| where | was |
|---|---|
| `ci.yml` ×3 | 20 (EOL) |
| `Dockerfile.ssg`, `Dockerfile.ssg-worker` | 20 (EOL; the worker is in production) |
| `apps/web/Dockerfile`, `apps/admin/Dockerfile` | 22 |
| `mobile-ota.yml`, `mobile-release.yml` | 22 |
| `deploy.yml`, which builds the production frontend | **unpinned** — no `setup-node` at all |

Now `.nvmrc` holds `24` and everything reads it. Workflows use `node-version-file`; Dockerfiles
cannot read it, so they take `ARG NODE_VERSION` and `scripts/check-node-version.mjs` fails CI when
they drift. The check rejects a literal `node-version:` in a workflow even when the number happens to
be right — agreeing today is not the property being protected. Verified against both drifts that
actually happened, and that it exits non-zero rather than only printing.

Node 24 rather than 22: active LTS to 2028-04-30, and it closes a two-major gap between the machine
the code is written on and the one CI verified it with. Nothing caps it — React Native and Metro want
`>=20.19.4`, Vite `>=20`, eas-cli `>=22`.

`deploy.yml` is checked, not installed. That job has no `actions/checkout` — it works inside
`$PROJECT_DIR` — so `node-version-file` has nothing to read, and swapping the runtime under a
production deploy without being asked is worse than refusing to run. It compares the runner's Node
with `.nvmrc` after the pull and before the build, so a mismatch stops the deploy while it is still
only a git checkout.

`.nvmrc` holds `24.20.0`, not `24`, and that is the runner's doing. It resolves Node with **asdf**,
already configured with `legacy_version_file = yes`, so it reads `.nvmrc` — but literally: a file
saying `24` fails with *"No preset version installed for command node"* even with 24.20.0 installed.
Checked on the machine rather than assumed. Pinning the patch also means CI, both images and the
production build compile on one Node instead of on whatever each resolved `24` to that week.

The runner itself was the last thing anyone had looked at. It was on **Node 20.14.0** — end of life
since 2026-04-30, and the machine that builds the production frontend. (A first pass reported 18.20.8:
that is the snap on the interactive PATH, not what the runner uses, which is asdf's.) Node 24.20.0 is
now installed alongside, with pnpm provisioned under it; the global default is deliberately unchanged,
so `.nvmrc` in the repo is what selects the version for this project and nothing else on the box
moved.

Noticed while mapping this: `apps/web/Dockerfile` and `apps/web/Dockerfile.ssg` are referenced by
nothing — not `docker-compose.yml`, not the Makefile, not CI. `Dockerfile.ssg` is still described in
`docs/02-system/ssg-prerender.md` as the image carrying Chromium. Both were updated with the rest
rather than deleted, since deleting is a separate decision, but part of the "EOL Node in production"
alarm came from reading a file that nothing builds.

<a id="2026-09-01-ci-an-ota-goes-out-on-merge"></a>

## CI — an OTA goes out on merge, and refuses to when it would reach nobody — infra — 2026-09-01

Shipping a JS-only mobile change meant running `eas update` from a laptop. `mobile-release.yml`
already had an `ota-update` action; it went unused because the `EXPO_TOKEN` secret has never been
set, which is still the one thing blocking every EAS path in CI.

New workflow `mobile-ota.yml` publishes to the production channel on any push to `main` touching
`apps/mobile/**` or `packages/**`. Kept separate from `mobile-release.yml` rather than bolted onto
it: that file's header states it is `workflow_dispatch` only so nothing reaches the store without a
human clicking, and that remains true — an OTA republishes the JS bundle to apps already installed
and never touches the store.

The guard is the reason the file exists. `app.json` sets `runtimeVersion.policy: "fingerprint"`, so
an update only lands on builds whose runtime matches. When a merge moves a native dependency, a
config plugin or `app.json`, the runtime moves with it — and `eas update` **still succeeds**,
publishing into a runtime nobody has installed. A green check and zero delivery, with nothing else
in the system positioned to notice. It is the shape of the
[SSG rebuild that reported success after losing its files](../incidents/2026-08-31-deploy-wiped-a-running-ssg-rebuild.md),
and worse, because that one was eventually visible on the site.

So the workflow resolves the runtime, compares it with the newest finished Android production build,
and publishes only on a match. A mismatch **fails the run** with what to do instead. Failing is the
deliberate choice: a skipped step and a delivered update look identical in a green tick.

Two things are decided rather than guessed, since neither can be tested before the token exists: the
production build is selected in `jq` on `buildProfile`/`channel` rather than through a CLI filter
flag that has been spelled both `--profile` and `--buildProfile` across EAS versions, and the missing
token is detected by reading it from `env` rather than a step-level `if`, where the `secrets` context
is not dependably available. The commit message reaches `eas update` through `env` too — it is
attacker-controlled text, and `${{ }}` inside a `run:` block executes what it contains.

`docs/03-ops/play-store-release.md` promised `npm run check:runtime-version` "if it has been wired
up". It never was; the runbook now points at the workflow that does it.

Untested end to end, and cannot be until `EXPO_TOKEN` is set — which is why `workflow_dispatch`
carries a `dry_run` input, defaulting to on, so the first run compares without publishing.

**Update, same day.** The token landed and the first run failed before reaching any of the above:
`eas-cli@23.2.0` pulls `@oclif/plugin-autocomplete`, which declares `engines.node >= 22`, and yarn
refuses to install it on the Node 20 both workflows pinned. `mobile-release.yml` carried the same
pin, so the manual OTA path had a second reason not to work beyond the missing secret — and neither
workflow had ever run far enough to find out. Both now use Node 22.

<a id="2026-09-01-beta-a-standing-android-badge"></a>

## Beta — a standing Android badge on the site and the README — web — 2026-09-01

`AndroidTesterBanner` already invites Android readers into the closed test, but deliberately
narrowly: an Android device, a reader who has already opened a book, once, dismissible. That is the
right shape for an invitation and the wrong shape for "this project has an app" — a visitor on a
laptop, or anyone reading the repo, never learns the app exists.

So: a badge in the site footer, shown to everyone, and one in the README's badge row plus a line
under the call-to-action.

**Not the official "Get it on Google Play" badge**, which was the obvious thing to copy. That badge
links to a store listing, and ours does not exist yet:

```
play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=app.textstack.mobile   →  404 Not Found
play.google.com/apps/testing/app.textstack.mobile            →  200
```

It would have sent every visitor to a Not Found page, and Google's brand guidelines scope the badge
to published apps in the first place. The footer badge borrows the shape — icon plus a two-line
lockup, so it reads as a store badge at a glance — and says what it is: ANDROID APP / Join the beta,
with "In closed testing — open to anyone with an Android phone" underneath. The day the production
track goes live, the link is the only thing that changes.

Built from the existing theme tokens, so it inverts with the rest of the footer; checked in both
light and dark.

<a id="2026-09-01-selection-extending-a-long-press-reaches-the-app"></a>

## Selection — extending a long-press into a sentence reaches the app — mobile — 2026-09-01

Found on an emulator while verifying the speech fix, and it turned out to be the defect that
actually decided what got read aloud. The reader could select a sentence and have Listen read a
single word — not because speech picked the wrong text, but because the app never heard about the
sentence.

After the 450 ms hold selects a word, dragging the handles out to a phrase fires `selectionchange`.
Every one of those events landed inside the 1500 ms suppression window the tap path arms, so all of
them were dropped and the app's selection stayed on the word. Listen, Translate and Explain all
acted on that word, and Ask stayed hidden because `isMultiWord` was still false.

Confirmed by nudging a selection handle *after* the window had expired: the identical event
delivered the full sentence immediately.

| | |
|---|---|
| word selected (long-press) | `12:18:18.349` |
| suppression window closes | `≈12:18:19.849` |
| debounced dispatch would fire | `≈12:18:19.82` |

Thirty milliseconds inside. That margin is why the report was "it behaves differently on different
phones" — a device that completes the drag slightly slower lands on the other side of the window and
works fine.

The release path built to prevent exactly this — `touchmove` zeroing the deadline once the finger
passes `SELECT_EXTEND_TOLERANCE` — carries the comment *"Needs on-device validation of the
extend-from-tap gesture"*. It was never validated, and it does not fire: once Android's selection
ActionMode owns the gesture, the page stops receiving `touchmove` at all.

The window now gates only what it was built for. A collapse inside it is the ActionMode going away;
an echo of a selection we made ourselves repeats text the existing dedupe already drops. Neither can
produce a *different, non-empty* selection, so that one is dispatched regardless.

Reproducing it needs `adb shell input motionevent`, not `input swipe`: swipe starts moving
immediately, the finger passes `DRAG_TOLERANCE` at 8 px, and the word timer is cancelled before it
fires — the gesture under test never happens. Full pass, with logs:
[`docs/qa/reports/2026-09-01-android-tts-selection.md`](../qa/reports/2026-09-01-android-tts-selection.md).

<a id="2026-09-01-reader-speech-starts-when-asked"></a>

## Reader — speech starts when asked, and the button reads what you picked — mobile — 2026-09-01

A tester on a Galaxy S24 reported that selecting a sentence and pressing Listen produced silence, or
a cut-off word, depending on timing. Tapping a single word worked. The phrasing that reached us was
"the voice breaks off", which pointed at the audio, and the audio turned out to be fine.

Production `/api/tts` was measured first, because a truncated MP3 would have explained everything:

| input | response | bytes | duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| 44 chars | 200 | 22 KB | 3.72 s |
| 375 chars | 200 | 123 KB | 20.52 s |

Whole file, 1.18 s to synthesise. Nothing server-side was cutting anything off. Three client
defects were stacking into one symptom.

**The word spoke itself.** A single-word selection auto-played. The message carrying it is posted by
the 450 ms long-press in `readerBridge.ts` — which is also the first frame of the drag that selects a
sentence. So the gesture that means "I am choosing a phrase" began by reading its first word aloud,
under a finger that had not finished moving. On a device with Samsung's own long-press timing this
lands differently than on a Pixel, which is why it looked device-specific.

**Then the button meant the wrong thing.** `toggle` branched on a single boolean — something is
playing, so stop. That is correct for the button that started the sound and wrong for every other
one. With the word still playing, pressing Listen on the sentence was read as "stop the word", and
the sentence never played at all. A second press worked, because by then nothing was playing. That
is the whole of "it works every other time".

**And the wait was invisible.** Fetching audio takes about a second on a first play. The button said
Listen throughout, so the natural response — press it again — cancelled the fetch that was already
running. Even with the comparison fixed, that press would still have read as stop.

The decision now lives in `apps/mobile/src/lib/ttsRequest.ts` as a pure function over
`(phase, currentText, requestedText)`, tested, following `pdfWritePolicy.ts`. It is a small piece of
logic to extract, and it is extracted because the path around it can only be exercised on a phone:
`apps/mobile/e2e` runs Chromium against `expo start --web`, where there is no `expo-audio` and no
native long-press, so nothing automated reaches the reader's speech path.

Auto-speak is gone rather than rescued. It was the root of the race, and unrequested audio during a
selection gesture is the thing the reader is supposed not to do. Speech now only ever starts from a
button. The web reader was already right on all three counts — `speak()` there begins with `stop()`,
and `isLoading` has always been separate from `isPlaying` — so this is mobile catching up to a
contract that already existed, not a new design.

Deleting the sentence button was considered and rejected: it would not have fixed anything. The word
would still have spoken itself mid-drag; there would simply have been no way left to hear the
sentence.

None of this was the whole story, which is the argument for testing a fix rather than reasoning it
closed. Verifying on an emulator showed the sentence was not reaching the app at all — see the entry
above. Speech had been reading the only text the app had.

<a id="2026-09-01-selection-a-long-passage-stops-disappearing"></a>

## Selection — a long passage stops disappearing instead of opening a toolbar — mobile — 2026-09-01

Found while tracing the speech bug. Selecting more than 300 characters posted no message to the app
at all, so no toolbar appeared — not just no Listen, but no Copy and no Highlight either, neither of
which has any length limit. From the reader's side the selection simply did nothing.

The number was wrong as well as the behaviour. `Tts:MaxTextLength` and
`OpenAI:Translate:MaxTextLength` are both 500, so a passage between 300 and 500 characters was
refused by the client on behalf of a server that would have accepted it.

The selection is now always dispatched, carrying whether it exceeds 500, and the toolbar disables
only the three actions that genuinely cannot work — Listen, Translate, Explain. Copy, Highlight and
Ask stay live.

The reader-html smoke test was repointed in the same change. It parsed `READER_OVERLAY_SCRIPT` out of
`readerOverlayScript.ts`, which became a one-line re-export of the generated bundle in April; the
marker has been absent and the script exiting non-zero, unnoticed, ever since — nothing runs it. It
now parses `READER_SELECTION_BRIDGE` instead, the 27 KB of hand-written JavaScript living inside a
TypeScript template string, which had no syntax check of any kind. A stray brace there is invisible
to `tsc` and arrives as a reader with no word tap, no selection toolbar and no highlights. Confirmed
it fails against a deliberately broken bridge rather than passing vacuously.

<a id="2026-08-31-search-a-common-word-stops-timing-out"></a>

## Search — a common word stops timing out, because ranking happens after deduplication — backend — 2026-08-31

Sentry reported two `NpgsqlException: Timeout during reading attempt` from `PostgresSearchProvider`
within the same second. Reproduced immediately: `?q=war` returned **500**, `?q=love` took 4.9s,
`?q=dracula` took 158ms. The provider's command timeout is 5s, so anything above it is a 500 to the
reader.

The index was not the problem, which is worth stating because it is where one looks first. The GIN
index on `chapters.search_vector` is valid, 91 MB, and matches 23,487 of 40,589 chapters for *love*
in 9ms. Measured against the same rows:

| | |
|---|---|
| match only | 9 ms |
| match + `ts_rank` + order | **7,747 ms** |
| `ts_headline` on the 20 output rows | 48 ms |

`ts_rank` reads the tsvector of every row it scores and no index can help it, so strategy 4 was
paying for 23,487 scores to return ten results. `ts_headline`, the usual suspect, is cheap here.

The obvious cheap fix is to cap the candidate rows, and it is a trap: 500 chapters in heap order are
consecutive chapters of the same few books, so ranking a 500-row cap finds 36 of the 1,334 books that
actually match. Deduplicating first costs a sort on `edition_id` and leaves exactly one row per book
— 1,334 scores instead of 23,487, same 1,334 books, 70 ms.

End to end against production, the whole CTE with all four strategies and headlines: **125 ms**, with
*Sons and Lovers*, *Women in Love* and *Love's Labour's Lost* at the top where they belong.

The trade is which chapter represents a book: the first match by `DISTINCT ON` rather than the
highest-scoring one, so an edition's score comes from one of its matching chapters rather than its
best. Recovering that would mean ranking all 23,487 again, which is the cost being removed.

Pre-existing, not new — it only ever surfaced on words common enough to match half the corpus, and
search sends this site one visitor a month. It became visible because an SSG rebuild put the box
under load and pushed a 4.9s query past 5s.

<a id="2026-08-31-android-the-launcher-icon-stops-being-a-solid-block"></a>

## Android — the launcher icon stops being a solid block for anyone using themed icons — mobile — 2026-08-31

The first closed-testing tester installed the app and reported two things. One of them was "и ярлык
чёрный".

An adaptive icon is layers, and Android composites them by alpha. Both of ours had none.
`android-icon-monochrome.png` was a 432×432 image that is 100% white with alpha 255 on every pixel —
blank, and fully opaque. Themed icons take that layer, read its alpha as the shape and fill it with
the wallpaper's accent: an alpha of 255 everywhere means the shape is the entire tile, so the
launcher drew a solid rectangle, dark on a dark theme. Nobody had seen it because themed icons are
opt-in and off by default; the tester had them on.

The same defect, less visibly, in `android-icon-foreground.png`: greyscale, no alpha, 91% white
around a dark T. Being opaque it covered the background layer completely, which is why
`adaptiveIcon.backgroundColor` (`#F3EEE7`) and `android-icon-background.png` had never once been
visible. The icon was a white tile that happened to look deliberate.

Both layers are now the T as a real silhouette: alpha 0 outside it, 255 inside, 8.8% coverage — the
foreground keeping the mark's own `#2B2B2B` so the configured background finally shows through, the
monochrome white because the system tints by alpha and ignores colour. Geometry is untouched; this
is the same T in the same place, cut out rather than painted on.

Icons are a native resource, so this needs a build — an OTA cannot carry it.

<a id="2026-08-31-beta-the-android-invite-works-on-the-live-site"></a>

## Beta — the Android invite works on the live site, where chapter URLs end in a slash — web — 2026-08-31

The banner shipped an hour earlier was inert in production, and the way it failed is the useful part.

Its one interesting rule — wait until the visitor has opened a book, never interrupt them while they are in one — is decided by a single pattern matched against the pathname, anchored on the chapter segment: `/books/[^/]+/[^/]+$`. Under `vite preview`, where it was verified, `/en/books/twelfth-night/2-act-i` is exactly what the router sees and the pattern matches. On textstack.app nginx 301s that URL to `/en/books/twelfth-night/2-act-i/`, and an anchored `[^/]+$` cannot match a path ending in a slash. So the read flag was never recorded, the banner never appeared for anyone, and — the half that would have been worse had the first half worked — it would not have hidden itself inside the reader either.

Both failures are the same missing character. The pattern now allows an optional trailing slash, and the negative case that keeps it honest is `/en/books/twelfth-night/` — the book page, also slash-terminated, which must still show the banner because it is not the reader.

A test now pins all of it: platform, the two preconditions, dismissal, and the reader paths in both slash forms. The bug was reachable only through a production URL shape that the local preview does not produce, which is the argument for the test rather than another manual pass.

<a id="2026-08-31-beta-the-site-invites-android-readers"></a>

## Beta — the site invites Android readers into the closed test, but only ones who have opened a book — web — 2026-08-31

The Android app needs twelve testers opted in for fourteen continuous days before Play unlocks production, and the account had four — all of them the owner's own. The obvious places to ask are the tester-swap groups on Telegram; the honest arithmetic says otherwise. Those groups hold a few hundred people between them, half of the posts are offers to sell console access, and everyone there is farming opt-ins rather than reading. The site takes 411 anonymous sessions a week and 52 of those visitors have saved a vocabulary word. The people already on the page are both more numerous and the right ones.

So the ask moved to the page. `AndroidTesterBanner` is the cookie banner's twin — same markup shape, same bottom-fixed layout, same dismissal-in-`localStorage` contract — and it links to the closed test's opt-in URL.

What is worth explaining is the condition it waits for. Play does not merely count testers: the production application requires a written summary of tester feedback, and Google lists "insufficient tester engagement" beside "fewer than 12 opted-in testers" as a reason to send an applicant back for more testing. A banner shown on arrival recruits people who install the app and never open it — which is not neutral, it is the specific failure that costs the application. So the banner holds until the visitor has been in the reader at least once, recorded as a flag on the way through. Someone who read a chapter and came back is the tester the application needs to be able to describe.

Two smaller rules follow from the same principle. It never appears inside the reader, because interrupting reading to advertise a reading app is self-defeating. And it stays hidden until the cookie banner has been answered, because both own the bottom of the screen and only one of them is legally obliged to be there.

The Android check is a `navigator.userAgent` test, which keeps it out of the prerendered HTML for free: the SSG worker drives headless Chrome with a desktop agent, so the banner never renders during prerender and no static page ships with it baked in.

<a id="2026-08-29-resume-the-locator-decides-on-every-screen"></a>

## Resume — the locator decides on every screen that offers to continue, not just one — mobile — 2026-08-29

`#496` established the rule and applied it in one place. A progress row's `chapterSlug` is not something any client sends: the API derives it by joining the row's `chapterId` to the chapters table, and `chapterId` stops moving the moment infinite scroll carries the reader past the chapter whose URL they opened. The locator, written from the chapter actually on screen, is the half that tracks reality. The book screen was taught to prefer it. The library shelf and the "Continue Reading" rail were not, and both were still reading `server.chapterSlug` straight off the DTO.

That is how QA's 45% became 0.66%. A row can hold `locator: scroll:4-act-iii:7488` with a `chapterId` still pointing at act one; the shelf opens act one; `loadPosition` requires the locator's slug to match the chapter being opened, finds it does not, and returns nothing; the reader lands at the top with "nothing to restore", which legitimately opens the write gate; and the first automatic save writes `scroll:2-act-i:0` over a half-read play. Every step is correct in isolation. The wrong turn was made on the first one.

The obstacle to fixing it was that neither screen has a chapter list — both work from a progress row alone — and `resumeChapterSlug` validated both candidates against a list before ranking them, so with no list it answered "no idea" and the callers fell back to the field it exists to distrust. The rule now separates ranking from validation: with a list, a candidate must name a chapter that exists; with no list, there is nothing to check against and the ranking is all there is, so the locator still wins and the stored slug is still the fallback. A slug that may be stale beats sending the reader back to chapter one. This also means passing a list that has not loaded yet behaves like passing none — the answer comes sooner and corrects itself when the list arrives, which on the uploaded-book screen replaces half a second of "Start Reading" on a book the reader is halfway through.

Also from the same report, and the same theme: the book screen now shows how far in the reader is. The shelf card said "11% complete" while this screen offered a bare "Continue Reading" — same reader, same book, one of the two screens keeping it to itself. It reuses `storedBookPercent` and `formatBookPercent` exactly as the uploaded-book screen already does.
<a id="2026-08-29-progress-a-row-stops-being-able-to-contradict-itself"></a>

## Progress — a row stops being able to contradict itself — mobile — 2026-08-29

The write side of the same rule, closing the way the contradiction could be created again. A progress row carries a locator and a chapter id, the server derives the chapter slug from the id, and the two must name the same chapter or every consumer of that slug is wrong in a way nothing on the row reveals.

`#496` made the id follow the chapter on screen by looking the visible slug up in the book's chapter list, and fell back to the route chapter's id when the list could not answer — reasoning that a position saved against a slightly stale chapter beats a position not saved. On a cold open that fallback is not stale at all: the reader has not moved, the visible slug *is* the route slug, and the route id is exactly right. The case it misses is the other one. Infinite scroll fetches `chapter.next` by itself and needs no chapter list, so a reader whose book request failed — offline, a transient error, a slow link — can spend an entire session moving through chapters while `chapters` stays empty, and every save pairs the route id with a locator naming a chapter further on.

So the fallback now asks the question that distinguishes them: is the reader still where the route put them? If yes, the route id is used as before. If not, the server write is held back rather than sent contradictory, and the local record — which is keyed and resumed by slug, and is the position either way — is written as usual. The deferred write goes out when the chapter list arrives, via a one-shot effect on the transition from no list to a list. That is a different signal from the deferred-save replay already in `useReaderPersistence`, which waits on the route chapter's own id and has long since fired by the time this matters.
<a id="2026-08-29-reader-a-restore-now-says-when-it-has-landed"></a>

## Reader — a restore now says when it has landed, and nothing is saved before it does — mobile — 2026-08-29

QA opened a book at 45%, pressed back inside a second, and the stored position was 0.66%. Twice, on both entry points, with the loss scaling to how far in the reader had got — 45.2% and 40.3% both collapsed to 0.66%, while 11% dropped 1.5 points at a time and kept dropping on each repeat. A normal fifteen-second visit drifted 21px, so the damage was specific to leaving early.

**The gate added the day before was guarding the wrong instant.** `#497` made the mobile reflow reader refuse to write a position until one had been restored, and set that flag in `tryRestore` — the function that *injects* `window.__textstackRestoreScroll` into the WebView. Injection is not arrival. The scroll happens a paint later in another JavaScript realm, and until it reports back, the newest position React Native holds is the `scrollY: 0` the page posts on its own `load` event. The gate was wide open across that entire window, so the unmount flush, the background flush and the debounce all had a zero to write. The restore functions were fire-and-forget and returned nothing; the only feedback was the scroll event they caused, which arrives as an ordinary `progress` message indistinguishable from the reader scrolling and is suppressed outright when the delta is under 0.005. There was nothing to wait for.

So the restore now acknowledges itself. Each one carries an id into the WebView and reports back under its own `restored` message with the offset it reached — the same shape the PDF viewer has used since `pdfPersistGate`, which was written after this same class of bug on the other reader: `scrollToPage(page, jumpId)` in, `ackJumpId` out. The two-state predicate becomes a three-phase machine, `awaiting → issued → open`, and `issued` is the state that did not exist before. It has three ways out, and each earns its place: an id-matched acknowledgement (the happy path, and the id is what stops an ack from the chapter just left opening the gate on the chapter just entered); any reported position above zero, which stands in for a lost acknowledgement because wherever the reader is, they are somewhere real — the load event's zero, deliberately, opens nothing; and a four-second settle timeout on a real timer, the same value and the same argument as `PDF_JUMP_SETTLE_MS`, because one injection that never arrives must not silence saving for the rest of the session. Losing the tail of one session is bad; losing every session is worse. "Nothing was saved" still opens the gate immediately — the top of the chapter is where that reader belongs, and treating it otherwise would mean a first read could never be persisted at all.

The percent branch got a function of its own while the ack was being added. It had been a raw string injected from the hook with no counterpart in the page, which is precisely why it had nowhere to report from; both branches now go through the same helper and the same acknowledgement, and the settings-rebuild re-apply reuses it too. One incidental trap worth recording: the reader document is a single TypeScript template literal, so a backtick in a comment inside it ends the string — the first version of this change did exactly that, and `tsc` reported it as a module declaration error two lines away from the real cause.

Not fixed, deliberately: the report also notes ~75px lost on each re-entry, about 0.05% a time. The likely cause is `scrollTo` under-shooting before the document height has settled — the PDF path handles it by checking page dimensions before acking — but a re-application loop is a new source of races bought for a twentieth of a percent. The acknowledgement now carries the achieved offset, so it is at least measurable when it matters.

<a id="2026-08-28-mobile-the-language-question-is-decided-while-rendering"></a>

## Mobile — the language question is decided while rendering, not by an effect that could be missed — mobile — 2026-08-28

Third reproduction, third fix. A brand-new account on a cleared install lands on Library with no language question; `GET /me/profile` says `nativeLanguage: null`, so asking was required; the question then appears on the next cold start. Identical to the two runs before it. **The reason three attempts produced no diagnosis is mine:** the instrumentation written to settle it — three `console.log` calls added in #474 — sits behind `__DEV__`, and every reproduction ran a release build from Play plus an OTA, where `__DEV__` is false. A question that could not answer itself, asked three times. Worse, one of the two plausible mechanisms was also mine: `router.dismissAll()` before `replace()` was added in #474, i.e. after the second reproduction, and expo-router queues both, computing the REPLACE against a route tree that has not yet absorbed the POP_TO_TOP dispatched in the same drain. So the shape changes rather than the guess. The gate was a null-rendering sibling of the root `<Stack>` deciding inside a `useEffect` with two early returns, one of which tested a `pathname` that is stale at exactly the moment registration navigates. It is now a decision taken during render, in the tabs layout, where the reader actually lands — `<Redirect>` when the answer is "ask", the tabs when it is not, and the tabs while it is still unknown. A render-time decision cannot be skipped the way an effect can, and it has to live inside a screen subtree because expo-router's `<Redirect>` is built on `useFocusEffect`, which needs a focus context a sibling of the navigator does not have. The `alreadyOnboarding` guard became structural: the onboarding route is outside the tabs, so the layout is not mounted while it shows. `landAfterAuth` keeps one job — leave the auth modal — because two copies of the rule is how it drifted. Evidence now survives a release build: a Sentry breadcrumb records the five inputs and the answer, so a fourth reproduction would finally leave a trace. Found while tracing and fixed alongside, because it is not an onboarding bug at all: `useReaderVocabActions` still held `nativeLanguage !== language ? nativeLanguage : 'en'` — the exact fallback `useTargetLanguage` was rewritten to delete — so saving a word from an English book with no native language set produced the card `lucius → Lucius`. A word with nothing to translate into is now saved as it is.

<a id="2026-08-28-mobile-a-book-screen-re-reads-where-you-got-to"></a>

## Mobile — a book screen re-reads where you got to, instead of showing where you were before you read — mobile — 2026-08-28

Read a PDF to page 24, press back, and the book screen showed a dash where the percentage goes, a greyed-out progress bar, and a button saying "Start Reading" — while the server already held `{"locator":"page:24","percent":0.197}`. Leaving the screen and returning fixed it. Three symptoms, one cause: `savedProgress` was still the `null` it was initialised with, because the reader is opened with `push` and left with `router.back()`, so the book screen is never unmounted, and its load effect is keyed on `[id, reconnects, attempt]` — none of which change when a reading session ends. It is the first thing you see on closing a book, so it is not a defect anyone can miss. The fix is the pattern the repo already had and had already written the rationale for: `useContinueReadingList` recomputes on focus, noting that "the only thing that changes these values is the user leaving the reader, which is a focus event". Only the progress is re-fetched — a book's own fields do not change by reading it — and a failed refresh keeps whatever the screen already shows rather than blanking a good value. Found next door while fixing it: the catalog book screen set its resume slug only `if (p?.chapterSlug)`, and a PDF read in Original layout is chapterless, so a half-read catalog PDF said "Start Reading" even after a clean mount. The user-book screen had carried a fallback for exactly this since the Original-first work; the catalog screen never got one. That rule is now `resumeChapterSlug` in `packages/shared`, with the dangling-slug case tested, because two copies of a resume rule means one of them is wrong and nothing says which.

<a id="2026-08-28-four-small-places-where-the-screen-said-more"></a>

## Reader + highlights + tutor — four small places where the screen said more than the data did — backend + web + mobile — 2026-08-28

**Alignment.** The reader opened books ragged-right on mobile and justified-centre on web — the same setting with two different defaults since both shipped. Mobile now matches web, and the drawer keeps all three options for anyone who disagrees. **Highlight context cut mid-word** — mine, from the day before. An anchor stores a raw 30-character window either side of the passage, and I rendered it verbatim, so QA read "…f the weather, with the signs **in** heaven and earth that fore-bo…": the quote begins on the tail of "of" and ends on the head of "forebode". The window itself cannot change — both anchor creators must agree on its size or a highlight made on one client matches worse on the other — so the trimming happens at render. The leading run always goes, because nothing in the text distinguishes the word "the" from the tail of "breathe"; losing one word of context is invisible, starting a quote mid-word is not. A window with no whitespace at all yields nothing rather than a fragment. **"You've seen all of these today"** was said at 00:08 about highlights seen at 14:47 the previous afternoon. The queue is a rolling 24-hour cooldown, not a calendar day, and the same file's docblock already said so — the copy disagreed with the comment above it. It now describes what the server actually does. **The tutor claimed a history that did not exist**, telling a reader "You keep missing this word" about a card created a minute earlier and never answered. Two causes, both ours: `get_due_vocabulary` did not project `totalReviews`, so a brand-new card and one answered wrong twice arrived identically — `consecutiveCorrect: 0`, differing only by a null accuracy that nothing explained; and the prompt's VOICE paragraph literally prescribed the sentence "You keep missing these two", with no condition attached. A prescribed phrase is a claim the model will make whether or not the data supports it. The tool now sends the count and the prompt names the rule: at zero answers the card is new, and saying otherwise is not allowed.

<a id="2026-08-28-mobile-the-library-filter-row-stopped-depending-on-fitting"></a>

## Mobile — the library filter row stopped depending on fitting a phone — mobile — 2026-08-28

A library with one book showed "All 1 · Reading 0 · Finished 0 · Not started" — three counts and then nothing, which QA read as numbers that do not add up. The numbers were never wrong: `countEntries` returns a full record with `notStarted` in it, tested, and the component renders the chip unconditionally. It was off the right edge of a horizontal ScrollView whose scroll indicator is hidden, so a number you could scroll to looked like a number that was missing. This was the third go at that row, and the file said so itself: the first attempt reordered the tabs and moved the clipping; the second tightened gaps and padding until four tabs plus four chips fitted the ~367dp left beside the view button on a 411dp phone — leaving nothing for a 393dp device, a 375dp one, or a reader who scales their system font. Both attempts were arithmetic against a budget that keeps changing. So the counts leave the tabs entirely. A tab filters; counting is not its job. The number of books you are actually looking at now sits above the list as one line, where it has room to be a sentence and says what you are looking at rather than what you are not. The squeezed metrics go back to ordinary, because a row that fits by arithmetic fits only the device the arithmetic was done on. The failed-uploads tab still appears only when it has something in it.

<a id="2026-08-28-vocabulary-a-card-says-its-word-out-loud"></a>

## Vocabulary — a card says its word out loud instead of waiting to be asked — mobile — 2026-08-28

The owner asked whether the flashcards had sound. They did: a speaker button on every card type, wired to the same TTS the reader uses, and QA verified it at the audio layer rather than by ear — a fourth `AudioTrack` at 24 kHz opening on the process at each tap, with the HAL going to standby after, which is what a stream that actually played looks like. What did not exist was any path where the word is spoken without being asked for: a repo-wide search for `autoSpeak`, `autoPlay` or `autoPronounce` returned nothing, and every card takes speech as an `onSpeak` prop bound to a press. So on the screen whose entire purpose is learning a word, hearing it was a thing you had to notice a small icon to get. It now speaks when the card appears, once per card — keyed on the card, so revealing the answer or re-rendering does not repeat it — with a toggle beside "Auto-save words on tap" in the reader settings, where `ttsSpeed` already lives. `speak` rather than `toggle`, because toggle would stop a previous card's audio if it happened to still be playing, and silence is not what someone who turned this on asked for. A device setting, not an account one, like everything else in that sheet.

<a id="2026-08-28-reader-stopped-writing-a-position-before-restoring-one"></a>

## Reader — the mobile reader stopped writing a position before it had restored one — mobile — 2026-08-28

The other half of the same data loss. Routing a reader to the wrong chapter is recoverable; what made it permanent is that the app then saved that wrong place over the right one, with nobody touching the screen. The WebView posts a `progress` message on its own `load` event carrying `scrollY: 0`, and on the mobile reflow path nothing stood between that and the server — `saveProgress` checked `enabled`, `bookKey` and `chapterSlug`, none of which say whether the reader is where they chose to be. A `restoredRef` existed and no writer read it. Two seconds later, a PUT of zero. The same window opened three other ways: any position load slower than the debounce, leaving a book within two seconds of opening it, and backgrounding the app in that gap — the unmount and AppState flushes were equally ungated. Both of this codebase's other readers already had the answer. The PDF viewer has `pdfPersistGate`, written after exactly this bug in its own form; the web reader keeps `restoredFor` as state rather than a ref, precisely so the save-on-open effect can re-run *after* restore, its comment insisting the offset must reflect the restored scroll "not a transient 0". The mobile reflow reader is simply the one that never got it, and now has it, keyed per chapter because moving on starts a new restore and a single boolean would stay open across the boundary. The gate is a pure function in `src/lib` — where mobile's tests can reach it — and a type predicate rather than a boolean, so extracting the check did not cost a duplicate one beside it. Deliberately not a rule against writing a smaller number: going back is something readers do, and the question is *when* a write is trustworthy, not which way the number moved.
<a id="2026-08-28-settings-a-stored-default-stopped-masquerading"></a>

## Settings — a stored default stopped masquerading as a decision, and auto-speak moved to where it is looked for — backend + mobile — 2026-08-28

QA reported the reader's text alignment still defaulting to left, a day after it was changed to centre. Both were true: the default did change, and their device had a stored `reader.settings.v2` blob from before, which the loader spreads over it. The deeper problem is that the two cases are byte-identical in storage — "the reader chose left" and "left was the default when this device first wrote anything" — because every `update` serialised the whole ten-key object, so adjusting the font size froze every other setting at that day's defaults. A changed default could therefore never reach anyone who had ever touched any setting. Only the touched keys are stored now: a new default reaches everyone who never expressed an opinion, and overrides nobody who did. Existing blobs carry no provenance and cannot be reconstructed, so they are honoured in full — which means one manual switch for anyone already on the old alignment, and correct behaviour from here. Alongside: the auto-speak toggle added yesterday sat in the reader's settings drawer while the feature runs on the review screen, two tabs away. QA said people would look for it where the cards are. It moved into the vocabulary settings sheet, and became an account setting rather than a device one — that sheet is server-backed by construction, with a save/cancel contract a device-local toggle would break, and speaking a word is a preference about how you learn rather than about this phone. The migration sets existing rows to true rather than the CLR default, so an account that already has a settings row gets the same behaviour as a new one.
<a id="2026-08-28-reader-continuing-a-book-stopped-erasing-where-you-got-to"></a>

## Reader — continuing a book stopped erasing where you had got to — mobile — 2026-08-28

Read to 10% of chapter two, leave, press "Continue Reading": the reader opened chapter *one* at the top, and seconds later the server held `scroll:1-dramatis-personae:0`, percent 0. The place was gone. Three reproductions, both entry points. The tester guessed the client was sending a stale `chapterSlug`; **no client sends `chapterSlug` at all.** The server derives it by joining the progress row's `chapterId` to the chapters table — and `chapterId` was the chapter the URL named, while the locator was built from the chapter on screen. Mobile's reflow reader appends the next chapter into the same document and never renavigates, so from the moment a reader crosses a chapter boundary the row disagrees with itself: locator in chapter two, id still in chapter one. That split has been there since June; what made it destructive was believing the wrong half on the way back in. The locator is the position and the slug is a projection of a field that stops moving, so the locator now decides — `resumeChapterSlug` reads the slug out of a `scroll:<slug>:<offset>` locator and prefers it, falling back to the stored chapter only when the locator names one that no longer exists (re-parsing renames chapters) or cannot answer at all (a chapterless PDF stores `page:<N>`). That alone repairs rows already written, because their locators were right the whole time. The write side is fixed where the disagreement starts: the id sent with a save is now resolved from the visible chapter's slug, falling back to the route id when the slug names nothing known — a position saved against a slightly stale chapter is worth more than a position not saved. The function also left `pdfResume.ts` for a file that is not named after one format, since living in a PDF-named file is how it came to consider only `page:<N>` locators to begin with.

<a id="2026-08-28-ai-tools-three-ways-an-agent-could-state-something"></a>

## AI tools — three ways an agent could state something the data did not say — backend — 2026-08-28

The tutor telling a reader "You keep missing this word" about a card created a minute earlier turned out not to be one bug. An audit of all eleven agent tools found the same shape repeatedly — **the projection drops the field that distinguishes two states the model is asked to narrate, and the tool's own description asserts the stronger of the two.** Three were fixed here. **The history claim was only half-closed the day before:** the prompt rule guarded `totalReviews == 0`, but a card answered twice and missed twice arrives with `lastAccuracy: 0.0` and sails past it — and `get_weak_vocabulary` described its own results as "the words they keep getting wrong" when the query has no accuracy threshold at all, only an ordering. For a learner whose worst card sits at 90% that description is simply false, and it lives where no prompt fix can reach it. The tool now says it ranks rather than judges, sends `lastReviewedAt` (two misses this morning and two last spring are the same accuracy and a different sentence), and the prompt rule covers "no pattern yet" rather than only "never answered". **Row truncation was invisible while string truncation was not:** `ToolJson.Truncate` has always returned a `truncated` flag that every string-returning tool surfaces, but five row-returning tools did `.Take(limit)` and reported `count = items.Count` — so "12 due" and "the first 12 of 400" were the same JSON, under a field name that asserts the former. They now return `returned` and `hasMore`, fetching one extra row rather than paying for a second COUNT. **And chapter numbers were wrong twice over:** `ChapterNumber` is 0-based, so every reader-facing surface adds one, and long chapters are split into parts that each get their own sequential number with the book's real number preserved elsewhere. Five tools handed the raw ordinal to the model, which cited it — the one part of an AI answer a reader can check by hand, and it pointed at the wrong chapter. Tools now emit a label ("Chapter 8", "Chapter 5 (part 2 of 3)") and the raw ordinal does not leave the server, because there is nothing a model can correctly do with it. One non-truthfulness finding is fixed here too, being a hole in a defence the project had already built deliberately: `get_example_sentence` emitted `bookTitle` unsanitized while `sentence` and the retrieved text beside it were cleaned, and a user-uploaded title is user-controlled text landing in the planner prompt as a tool observation — the sibling tool that sanitizes titles carries a comment saying to treat them as data and never as instructions. What the audit found and this did not fix is written down in STATUS rather than left in a transcript.

<a id="2026-08-28-vocabulary-an-answer-is-recorded-when-it-is-given"></a>

## Vocabulary — a Smart session answer is recorded when it is given, not when the session ends — backend + web + mobile — 2026-08-28

QA noticed it while confirming that Smart session answers finally reach spaced repetition at all: after answering the first of two cards the server still showed the old state, and both words moved together after "Session complete". That is because the tutor's only write happened inside the feedback turn, which the clients send when the last card is answered. So leaving a session halfway discarded the work already done — a loss that did not exist a day earlier, because a day earlier the work was discarded either way. `POST /me/tutor/session/{id}/answer` records one answer through the same `VocabularyReviewRecorder` and marks the same applied-word set the feedback path uses, so the closing re-plan still runs and still writes nothing twice. No planner and no LLM call on this route: an answer must not wait on planning to be worth anything, and re-planning per card would be several model calls a session. It carries the feedback path's trust rule too — an id the session never showed is not an answer to it, and is refused rather than recorded. Both clients post it fire-and-forget, because the card has already moved on and a failed write is recovered by the closing call that re-sends everything; the web hook's tests now pin both halves, including that a rejected write does not stall the card.

<a id="2026-08-27-vocabulary-smart-session-answers-now-count"></a>

## Vocabulary — Smart session answers now count — backend — 2026-08-27

The third QA pass answered one card "Knew" in Smart session, watched the summary say "Studied 1 · Accuracy 100%", and then read the word back from the API: `stage: 0`, `consecutiveCorrect: 0`, `nextReviewAt` still in the past, `dueNow: 1`. The card was offered again as a NEW WORD moments later. The control that made it a defect rather than a misreading: the same card answered in ordinary Practice moved `stage: 0 → 1` and pushed the next review out a day. So spaced repetition worked; the flagship AI mode simply never wrote to it, while reporting perfect accuracy over work it discarded. `ISrsEngine` was not injected into `TutorEndpoints` at all — the feedback handler read the learner's answers, planned the next turn from them, and persisted only `session.*`. Not even a `VocabularyReview` row, so a Smart session did not count toward the streak either. Both clients had the same gap (the mobile hook says in its own header that it is a port of the web one), which is why the fix is on the server: an installed build 22 starts counting on the next deploy, with no OTA. The write moved out of `SubmitReview` into `VocabularyReviewRecorder` unchanged, and both endpoints now call it, so "what an answer does" has one definition. The session row gained an applied-word set, and that is not defensive decoration: both hooks accumulate results in a ref that is never cleared, so turn two re-sends turn one's answers, and without the set a single "Knew" would have advanced the same card twice and pushed its next review a day further out than the learner earned. Found while wiring it and left alone deliberately: `selfAssessment` is accepted by `SubmitReviewRequest` and read nowhere on the server, so "Almost" and "Forgot" are already indistinguishable in ordinary Practice too — forwarding it from the tutor would have added a second sender of a field nobody reads.

<a id="2026-08-27-highlights-a-saved-passage-is-shown-inside-the-sentence"></a>

## Highlights — a saved passage is shown inside the sentence it came from, and "review" stopped claiming to be one — backend + web + mobile — 2026-08-27

QA highlighted a single word and every surface rendered it as `"in"` — the reader sheet, the Highlights list, the review card. Nothing about that quote could remind anyone why they saved it. The vocabulary list had solved the same problem a day earlier by showing the sentence with the word emphasised, and the fix had not reached highlights. **The context turned out to be already stored.** A reflow highlight saves a `{prefix, exact, suffix}` anchor with ~30 characters of the real page on each side; `HighlightDto` returns it, and `HighlightListItemDto` and `HighlightReviewDto` simply did not — so the field was dropped at the projection, not at capture, and every existing highlight gains its surroundings retroactively with no migration. The rendering reuses the vocabulary treatment rather than growing a second one: `contextSnippet` moved to `packages/shared`, where its trimming was split from its searching, because a highlight already knows where its passage is and only a word has to be found. PDF-rect anchors and the old no-anchor save path carry no surroundings and cannot be given any; those render as the passage alone, not as an empty frame — the helper returns null for exactly that reason, the same way it does when a vocabulary word is missing from its stored sentence. **The other half is a rename.** The screen called "Review Highlights" showed a passage and a Next button: no question, no recall, no self-assessment, and a summary that nonetheless said "Reviewed 2 highlights". The server cannot hold an answer either — `Highlight` has a single `LastReviewedAt`, the queue is a 24-hour cooldown, and the mark-reviewed request has no field a grade could arrive in. Mobile did not even have the flip that web has; its stylesheet carried a `flipHint` naming one. So it says what it does: Revisit, "You revisited N highlights", and the lightning-bolt icon that promised a drill is now an accessible label describing a page-turner. Real recall for highlights is recorded in STATUS as deliberately not done. Found alongside: the summary counter incremented outside its own `try`, so a run with no network still finished by claiming highlights the server had never heard of.

<a id="2026-08-27-ask-librarian-a-citation-names-its-chapter"></a>

## Ask + Librarian — a citation names its chapter, and the AI screens speak to the reader instead of about them — backend + web + mobile — 2026-08-27

Two defects on the AI surfaces, one shape: the internals reaching the screen. **"ch.0", six times.** An answer cited `[1][6][7][13][14][17]`, six chips appeared under it, and every one read `ch.0` while the chapter was titled "Book I" — so no marker could be matched to any chip. It was tempting to read that as a null leaking through, and it was not: `ChapterOrd` is genuinely 0-based (`XhtmlChapterParser` starts at 0, `IngestionService` copies `Order` to `ChapterNumber`, `BookChunkingService` copies that to the chunk), so `ch.0` really is the first chapter. The chip was simply the one surface printing the stored ordinal — the table of contents renders `chapterNumber + 1`, the footer renders `index + 1`, and the chapter's title was on the client the whole time, matched by the same `citationChapterSlug` that already powers tapping a chip to navigate. It now names the chapter. The second half is the marker: `AskCitation.marker` carries the `[n]` from the answer, mobile discarded it, and mobile deliberately does not make inline markers tappable — so the chip was the only place the two could ever have been joined, and it now leads with it. Web had the identical `ch.{ord}` label through i18n; both clients now share one helper, because a chapter title, a page number and an ordinal are not text that wants translating. **The other leak is a voice.** The librarian told a reader "The user requested short Roman classics about war… No external resources were needed as the library had sufficient on-topic and on-constraint books", and Smart session offered "…keeps the session concise and connected to the learner's recent reading". Both fields are shown on purpose — `TutorPlanView` says so in its own header, "the visible reasoning is the point, not debug text" — and both sit under a heading written in the second person ("Here's your plan, and why"). Nothing in either prompt constrained the register, and the prompts themselves say "the learner" and "the reader" throughout, so the model matched them. Tellingly, both hand-written fallback strings were already first-person: the intended voice existed everywhere except where the model could read it. Fixed in the two prompts rather than in the four screens that render them.

<a id="2026-08-27-mobile-counts-agree-with-their-nouns"></a>

## Mobile — counts agree with their nouns, the streak card stops filling four tabs, and a one-option control is gone — web + mobile — 2026-08-27

Three small things QA could see on one screen each. **"1 words added · 1 words reviewed."** The app had no way to write that correctly: `t(lang, key)` takes a key and nothing else — no parameters, no plural forms — so a count can never reach a translated string. What stood in for a mechanism was five hand-rolled ternaries in five files and thirteen places that hardcoded the plural. `plural(n, one, many, template)` in `packages/shared` replaces both, deliberately covering only English one-vs-many, which is the whole of what ships; real plural categories wait on `t()` accepting parameters, and that limit is now written down rather than rediscovered. Two of the thirteen turned out not to be plural bugs at all and were left alone: "~4 min" is an abbreviation, and "12-day streak" is attributive English, where the noun stays singular however long the streak — the hyphen was the missing piece there, not the s. **The streak card on all four Stats tabs.** It renders once, from a single JSX site — but inside the ScrollView and below the TabBar, under a comment claiming it was "always visible above tabs". It was not above anything; it simply occupied the top of every tab, and at roughly 550pt (a fixed 120pt chart plus a 365-cell heatmap) that is more than half a phone viewport, so on Achievements the first achievement sat below the fold. Web renders it once too, but genuinely above its tab bar — a layout with room a phone does not have. It now belongs to Overview, where the reader's other totals are. **The Language row that did nothing.** A chip row built from `supportedLanguages`, which is `['en']`: one chip, permanently selected, and tapping it called `switchLanguage('en')`, a no-op write of the value it already held. It sat two rows above Theme, where three chips of exactly that shape do work, which is what made it read as broken rather than as "only one locale so far". The precedent was already in the same file six lines below, where "Learning" had been deleted for being the identical thing — with a comment saying a control with one option is not a control. This is that comment again, for the row that was left. The native-language setting next to it is untouched: it is real and it works.

<a id="2026-08-27-deep-links-the-site-can-now-vouch-for-the-android-app"></a>

## Deep links — the site can now vouch for the Android app — web + docs — 2026-08-27

`adb shell am start -d "https://textstack.app/en/books/the-aeneid"` opened Chrome on a device with the app installed, and `pm get-app-links app.textstack.mobile` said why: `textstack.app: 1024`, verification failed. `app.json` has declared `autoVerify: true` for `/en/books*` and `/reset-password*` since the Play launch; Android duly fetched `https://textstack.app/.well-known/assetlinks.json` and was handed the SPA's `index.html` with a 200, because nginx's catch-all `try_files $uri /index.html` answers every unknown path that way — the same soft-404 behaviour already recorded in STATUS as a crawler problem, wearing a second hat. So every https link anyone could share — a book from a message, the password-reset email — walked past the installed app. The custom scheme was fine throughout and needs no verification, which is why nobody noticed. **No nginx change was needed:** `/.well-known/…` matches none of the regex locations and falls through to `location /`, whose `try_files $uri` serves a real file when one exists. The statement file therefore goes in `apps/web/public/`, not onto the server by hand — vite builds with `emptyOutDir`, so anything hand-placed in `dist/` dies at the next deploy, while `public/` is re-copied every build. **The fingerprint is the one step this repo cannot take:** the AAB is signed by Play App Signing, so the SHA-256 lives with Google and EAS. The file ships with a named placeholder and `docs/03-ops/android-app-links.md` says where to get the value, why both the app-signing and upload keys should be listed, and how to confirm with `pm verify-app-links --re-verify`. The test was going to be deliberately red to keep the task visible, which would have blocked every unrelated PR — it instead pins the file's shape now and switches its fingerprint-format check on by itself the moment the placeholder is replaced, so nobody has to remember a second commit to "turn on the test". Also found: iOS declares no `associatedDomains` at all and its entitlements file is empty, so Universal Links there were never configured on either side; recorded rather than fixed, Android being the launch platform.

<a id="2026-08-27-progress-a-percentage-now-travels-with-the-unit-it-is-mea"></a>

## Progress — a percentage now travels with the unit it is measured in — backend + web + mobile — 2026-08-27

`ReadingProgress.Percent` and `UserBook.ProgressPercent` are book-wide fractions, and for a long time nothing said so: mobile wrote the fraction of the current chapter, web wrote the fraction of the book, both landed in the same column. The 2026-08-26 QA pass caught the result on one screen without scrolling — the resume card said "The Aeneid — 10% complete" while the same book's row below said 32%, and the server's value was `0.31724`, the fraction read *inside Book II*. #454 fixed the clients, and that did not end it: the column had just been reset by migration and build 21, still running the old JS, filled it straight back in. An installed Android build keeps writing the old unit until its owner updates, and nothing about the number distinguishes the two. So the unit now travels with the value: a client that knows says `percentUnit: "book"`, and a percentage arriving without a declared unit is not stored. Untrusted writes are **not** rejected — the locator, chapter and timestamp are the reader's real position and are saved as always; only the number is left alone, and completion (derived from the number) cannot be triggered by one. A stale build keeps working and keeps its place; it just stops corrupting the column. Applied at both write paths (`UserDataEndpoints.ApplyProgressUpdate`, `UserBookService.UpsertProgressAsync`) and declared by every writer in one place each. Seven existing tests failed the moment the guard landed — they encoded the old contract, in which any number was storable — and were updated to declare their unit rather than deleted, because each represents a current client. Three new tests cover what they used to cover by accident, plus five on the predicate including a `"chapter"` unit that must never be trusted.

<a id="2026-08-27-reader-the-webview-stopped-reloading-itself-out-from-unde"></a>

## Reader — the WebView stopped reloading itself out from under the reader — mobile — 2026-08-27

QA read to page 17 of a 122-page PDF, left, and the server held `{"locator":"page:1","percent":0.0082}`. Four independent causes, and the largest was not the obvious one. **The document was reloading unprompted:** safe-area insets were dependencies of the `useMemo` that builds the viewer HTML, the reader renders `<StatusBar hidden={!barsVisible}>`, and on Android hiding the status bar changes `insets.top` — while `useReaderBars` auto-hides three seconds after open and toggles on every change of scroll direction. The document was torn down and rebuilt dozens of times per session with no user action, each time reopening at page 1 and reporting it. Reader theme was in the same list, so switching to dark did it too. The rule now: the template takes only values that cannot change while the document is open, and insets and theme travel to the live DOM over `injectJavaScript`. **The persist gate opened before the jump landed:** a boolean set at line 374, the target computed at 375-380, the fire-and-forget scroll issued at 381 — so the viewer's page-1 report passed the guard whose own comment said it existed to stop that, and nothing ever reset it. Replaced by a reducer over one document's life (`awaitingTarget → jumping → live`); a monotonic guard was considered and rejected because it would have masked the bug while breaking re-reading and backward navigation, and there is a test named for a reader turning back to page 3 that says so. **The viewer clobbered its own jump:** `prefetchDims` ran a self-jump to `openPage` on its first iteration, overwriting the target RN had set milliseconds earlier; `openPage` is now a seed for that same variable, leaving nothing to clobber it with, and jumps carry an id echoed back on the page report. **The write policy never fired for a fast reader:** the debounce was re-armed on every page tick, so skimming reached a flush only on close. The same reload bug was then found in the reflow reader, where scroll restore had been hiding it as a flicker — what it actually costs there is every chapter infinite scroll had appended. `check:mobile-pdf` added, mirroring the overlay guard the PDF bundle never had; while verifying it, both guards turned out to be cwd-sensitive (esbuild writes source paths inside the bundle relative to the working directory), so a clean tree could be reported as out of date. Both now pin `absWorkingDir`.

<a id="2026-08-27-mobile-the-app-asks-which-language-you-know-instead-of-tr"></a>

## Mobile — the app asks which language you know, instead of translating English into English — mobile — 2026-08-27

A five-minute-old account showed "I know: English / Learning: English" while the server said `nativeLanguage: null`, and tapping a word in an English book returned a Translation panel with two identical sections both labelled ENGLISH. The tester proved the size of it by setting Ukrainian by hand: the same toolbar that had shown five unlabelled icons then showed `goddess → богиня` inline, with highlight colours and "+ to vocabulary". TextStack's whole thesis is learning a language by reading real books in it, and the native language is the one input that thesis cannot run without — it is the target of every translation, gloss and vocabulary card. The app never asked. Now it asks once, between registering and the library. **One question, not two:** the report proposed asking what you know and what you are learning, but `TARGET_LANGUAGES` was `NATIVE_LANGUAGES.filter(code === 'en')` — one entry — and a question with one possible answer is not a question, it is the dead "Learning: English" chip the same report filed separately. That row and the state behind it are deleted. **No skip:** skipping restores the exact state the screen exists to end, a guessed value indistinguishable from a chosen one, and English is one tap at the top of Popular. The decision itself is a pure function with nine tests, because it has five inputs, four of them asynchronous, and both failure modes are invisible in review — a returning reader interrogated on every launch, or a new one never asked. `useTargetLanguage` was returning one value for two different questions and fell back to `'en'` when native matched the source, under a docblock promising "a sensible fallback so we never translate en → en"; it now returns `readerLang` (always real) and `translationTarget` (null when there is nothing to translate into), because null cannot be quietly passed to a translate call the way `'en'` could.

<a id="2026-08-27-mobile-offline-stopped-looking-like-an-empty-account"></a>

## Mobile — offline stopped looking like an empty account — mobile — 2026-08-27

Airplane mode, force-stop, launch: a library of twelve books rendered `FirstBookState` — "Your reader is ready. Add a book you already want to finish", the screen someone sees a minute after registering. No banner, no retry, nothing to distinguish it from having lost the account. The information was already in the app: `safeFetch` has set `isNetworkError` on every failed request since it was added, and the flag's own docblock says UIs "can show 'check your connection' instead of a generic server error". None did — the only mobile file reading it used it to suppress an abort, and Library, Discover and search each caught, logged to the console, and left their state at `[]`. Adds `isOfflineError` to `packages/shared`, with a docblock telling callers to check their own abort signal first, and a test for a 500: a server that answered badly is not offline, and telling that reader to check their wifi sends them away from the actual problem. The banner `app/book/[slug].tsx` already had — the one screen doing this correctly, rehydrating a `BookDetail` from SQLite — becomes a component. Library now distinguishes "no books" from "no answer" and falls back to downloaded books from the same cache, with a banner that says "downloaded books only" rather than implying the list is whole: uploads and saved-but-undownloaded books are not cached at all, and a convincing-looking partial list is worse than an honest one. Discover has nothing to fall back on and says so in one sentence instead of dropping three independently-gated sections. An offline search had been rendering as "No results for harry" — a claim about the catalog rather than the connection, which sends the reader off to doubt their spelling.

<a id="2026-08-27-mobile-controls-stopped-outranking-the-content-they-shape"></a>

## Mobile — controls stopped outranking the content they shape — mobile — 2026-08-27

Four screens, one illness, already diagnosed once when Library went from 13 blocks of chrome to 3. **Vocabulary** put eight blocks between the reader and their first word: a weekly budget bar, a settings chip, four to six stat tiles, an unlabelled two-colour segment (a review-mode switch), two large buttons, six filter chips, search, four sort chips, with "Mastered" wrapping to a second line. Same treatment: what stays is what changes the next action — one card with the due count and its button, search, the filter — and the rest goes behind one gear in a sheet built from `LibraryViewSheet`. The stat tiles were not wrong, they answered a question nobody had asked yet. **The Library filter** hid ten books and said nothing: the only surface showing the active source is inside a closed sheet, and — worse, and invisible to QA — the "Clear filter" button called `setStatus('all')` and nothing else, so in exactly the state the tester was in it visibly did nothing. **The reader** answered nothing back: tapping the page did not toggle the bars because the handler swallowed any tap landing on a word, and text covers almost the whole screen; the selected word's mark was a 650ms fade, so four unlabelled icons hovered over nothing; and a stripe ran across the last line of text because Android draws elevation from the native outline provider, which ignores an animated opacity, over a bar whose hide distance was hardcoded below its real height. **The Add-to-library menu** had five items, two labelled COMING IN A FUTURE UPDATE and a third that looked like it worked and opened the Chrome Web Store home page — the extension is in `extension/` at 0.1.0 and its own README records publishing as an unfinished owner-only step.

<a id="2026-08-27-user-books-an-uploaded-epub-opens-on-the-book-not-on-its"></a>

## User books — an uploaded EPUB opens on the book, not on its own index — extraction + mobile — 2026-08-27

Uploading a book is the app's best scenario — file to finished book with author, genre and description in under a minute — and its first screen was the book's table of contents, rendered in default-blue underlined links over the warm serif page, the title repeated three times, the numbering broken. Publishers routinely list the EPUB 3 navigation document in the spine, `EpubTextExtractor` walked the whole `ReadingOrder` with no filter, and `DetectFrontMatterType` politely titled the result "Contents". `UserIngestionService` carried a comment claiming TOC and front matter were filtered out — no such filter existed on that path, and the claim is why nobody went looking for one. Matched now by file path from the EPUB schema rather than by searching the text, because a real chapter is allowed to discuss a table of contents and that fuzzy match is exactly why the nav document passed for legitimate front matter; there is a test for that chapter, and the fixture is built inside the test because the defect is a structural choice a binary fixture would hide. Reader styling on the same screen: links had a colour and nothing else so the UA underline came through, and there were no `ol`/`ul`/`li` rules at all, which is why a book's numbered lists lost their numbers. PDF page navigation was a desktop control on a phone — a 56px numeric field showing a clipped "1 /" beside a Go button; the progress bar was already drawn and read-only, and can now be dragged, with the counter opening the numeric field when a precise page is what you want.

<a id="2026-08-27-mobile-the-newest-build-stopped-calling-itself-outdated"></a>

## Mobile — the newest build stopped calling itself outdated — mobile — 2026-08-27

The first thing a tester saw after installing from Play was an orange banner: "Update available — This version no longer receives updates. Install the latest from Google Play." Tapping Update opened a Play page offering only Uninstall or Open, because that *was* the latest build. `runtimeVersion.policy` was still `appVersion`, pinned at `1.0.0`, so every native build reported the legacy runtime and `LegacyRuntimeBanner` fired on all of them — including, on every cold start and offline too, the one it was telling people to install. The banner's own docblock had promised it would self-disable on builds carrying a fingerprint, and had said in capitals that the farewell OTA must ship *before* the policy change merged. The OTA shipped; the policy change did not, and `STATUS.md` still recorded the blocker as "the OTA has not been published" — which had stopped being true. Switching to `fingerprint` resolves locally to `c90afbb0…` over 102 native inputs, so the predicate returns false on the next build and the banner goes quiet by construction rather than by deletion, which is what lets it keep doing its real job for anyone stranded on an older binary. The test is the point: `isLegacyRuntime` had five unit tests and all five passed throughout the week the banner was lying, because the predicate was never wrong — its input was, and that comes from a config file no test read. Build 22 is the first TextStack build whose runtime is a fingerprint, which also means it is the first whose OTAs have a verified delivery target.

<a id="2026-08-26-mobile-library-was-13-blocks-of-chrome-before-the-first-b"></a>

## Mobile — Library was 13 blocks of chrome before the first book; now 3 — mobile — 2026-08-26

The owner sent a screenshot of a real device with 32 uploads: *"the interface is overloaded,
'Saved' means nothing, the user should see their books straight away."* Measured against the
code, the complaint was an understatement. The list header ran **~1571pt — about 2.4 screens
of chrome to scroll past before book one**, and three of the six sections above it were
themselves book-opening surfaces, so the reader met roughly three renderings of the same
library before reaching the actual list.

**The diagnosis: mobile flattened what web collapsed.** Web keeps one toolbar row (a sort
*menu*, a select toggle, a view switch), no shelves, and one merged list. Mobile had seven
rows of controls, three shelf carousels, and two lists. Worse,
[v3 slice 03](../ux-roadmap-v3/03-library-sidebar-source-filter.md) had required removing the
Saved/Uploads tabs in favour of a source filter — web did it, mobile shipped the drawer *and
kept the tabs*, so the app carried two mechanisms for the same state. This finishes that slice.

**Deleted.** The three shelf carousels — web deleted its own copies in July with the same
reasoning ("buggy and low-value, they push the actual book list below the fold"), and they were
three re-cuts of the same library. The "Jump back in" rail, which showed the reader's other
in-progress books as a second carousel over the same list the "Reading" filter produces in one
tap. The vocabulary card, which has its own tab and cannot be missed. The "Open filters" row,
the Saved/Uploads segment tabs, the dashed "Upload Book" button (the raised FAB was already the
entry point — there were three), and the storage quota bar, a settings concern sitting
mid-list. Roughly 1100pt of the 1571.

**Merged.** `SavedList` and `UploadsList` became one `BookList`. The saved/uploads split is a
storage detail — bookmarked catalog editions carry progress in a separate map, uploaded files
carry it inline — and it was surfaced as navigation, with table names for labels. The two lists
shared about 85% of their markup and drifted anyway. `packages/shared/src/library/entries.ts`
now tags each record with its `kind` and owns the accessors, status predicates, counts and the
single comparator, so both shapes interleave in one list and a row's kind decides only what it
can *show*: an upload can be mid-parse, a catalog edition cannot. Modelled on web's inline
`CombinedItem`, but extracted and unit-tested so the two platforms cannot drift again.

**Collapsed.** Source, sort, layout and collections moved into one `LibraryViewSheet` behind a
single options button — a bottom sheet rather than the old left drawer, so it opens next to the
thumb that asked for it. Five flat sort chips became a list inside it. "Recent" and "Recently
added" read almost identically and are now "Last read" and "Date added".

The header is now three blocks: resume card, search, one filter row. **The first book row is
visible without scrolling.**

Folded in because they live in the same rows: one status-badge system instead of two saying the
same words on the same line, one retry path for a failed upload (the ⋮ menu) instead of three,
and the failure reason kept as information rather than as a duplicate control.

118 mobile + 284 shared tests (26 new on the entries module: interleaving, key collisions
between an edition id and a book id, status semantics across both shapes, attention-pinning of
failed uploads, unparseable timestamps). Browser-verified light and dark at 390×844; the Android
bundle exports. Home and the tab bar are untouched.
